### CLoE: Curriculum Learning on Endoscopic Images for Robust MES Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13280v1)
- **Authors**: Zeynep Ozdemir, Hacer Yalim Keles, Omer Ozgur Tanriover
- **Abstract**: Estimating disease severity from endoscopic images is essential in assessing ulcerative colitis, where the Mayo Endoscopic Subscore (MES) is widely used to grade inflammation. However, MES classification remains challenging due to label noise from inter-observer variability and the ordinal nature of the score, which standard models often ignore. We propose CLoE, a curriculum learning framework that accounts for both label reliability and ordinal structure. Image quality, estimated via a lightweight model trained on Boston Bowel Preparation Scale (BBPS) labels, is used as a proxy for annotation confidence to order samples from easy (clean) to hard (noisy). This curriculum is further combined with ResizeMix augmentation to improve robustness. Experiments on the LIMUC and HyperKvasir datasets, using both CNNs and Transformers, show that CLoE consistently improves performance over strong supervised and self-supervised baselines. For instance, ConvNeXt-Tiny reaches 82.5\% accuracy and a QWK of 0.894 on LIMUC with low computational cost. These results highlight the potential of difficulty-aware training strategies for improving ordinal classification under label uncertainty. Code will be released at https://github.com/zeynepozdemir/CLoE.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CLOE (Curriculum Learning on Endoscopic Images), a novel curriculum learning framework designed to improve the robustness of Mayo Endoscopic Subscore (MES) classification for ulcerative colitis severity assessment using endoscopic images. CLOE addresses challenges like label noise, ordinal relationships between MES grades, and class imbalance. It utilizes image quality, estimated using a model trained on Boston Bowel Preparation Scale (BBPS) labels, as a proxy for sample difficulty. The framework then employs a curriculum learning strategy, starting with high-quality images and gradually introducing noisier samples. ResizeMix augmentation is incorporated to further enhance robustness. Experiments on the LIMUC and HyperKvasir datasets demonstrate that CLOE consistently improves performance over strong supervised and self-supervised baselines across various network architectures.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a relevant problem:** MES classification is clinically important, and the paper tackles well-known challenges associated with it (label noise, ordinality, class imbalance).
    *   **Novel application of curriculum learning:**  Applying curriculum learning to endoscopic image analysis for MES classification, guided by image quality as a difficulty proxy, is a relatively novel approach. Most existing curriculum learning frameworks in medical imaging do not explicitly account for ordinality or the impact of image quality.
    *   **Effective use of image quality proxy:** Using BBPS scores to estimate image quality is a clever workaround, as obtaining expert-defined uncertainty labels can be challenging and expensive.
    *   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The paper provides thorough experiments across multiple datasets, backbones, and baselines (both supervised and self-supervised). The inclusion of various metrics (Accuracy, Macro F1, QWK) is commendable and provides a holistic view of performance.
    *   **Demonstrated performance improvements:** The results clearly show that CLOE outperforms existing methods, particularly when combined with ResizeMix augmentation. The improvements in QWK highlight the framework's ability to better capture ordinal relationships.
    *   **Computational efficiency:** The focus on lightweight architectures demonstrates practicality for real-world clinical settings.
    *   **Code availability:** The promised code release adds to the reproducibility and potential impact of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Image-level split:** LIMUC dataset is used for training and testing on image-level split, which does not take the same patient's images into account. It may lead to a data leakage because images from the same patient can have similar characteristics.
    *   **Image quality proxy:** While a creative solution, image quality (via BBPS) may not always be a perfect proxy for sample difficulty.  Other factors (e.g., rare disease presentations, subtle visual cues) can also contribute to difficulty, independent of image clarity.
    *   **Limited ablation of curriculum design choices:** While the paper explores ResizeMix's impact, it could benefit from a more in-depth ablation of the curriculum learning schedule itself (e.g., exploring different thresholds, the number of phases, transition strategies).

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The primary novelty lies in the specific combination of curriculum learning, image quality-based difficulty estimation, and ResizeMix augmentation applied to the problem of MES classification with endoscopic images. While individual components (curriculum learning, image quality assessment, augmentation) are known, their integration within this context, particularly for ordinal classification under label noise, is a significant contribution. The consistent performance gains across multiple datasets and architectures suggest the method's generalizability.
    However, the reliance on BBPS as the proxy of quality could be seen as a limitation in broader contexts where a surrogate score like BBPS may not be available.

*   **Potential Impact:**

    The paper has the potential to influence research in medical image analysis, particularly in areas dealing with ordinal classification, label noise, and limited data. The practical focus and code release could facilitate adoption by other researchers and potentially lead to real-world clinical applications.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and effective approach to address a relevant problem in medical image analysis. The use of curriculum learning guided by image quality and combined with ResizeMix is a clever and well-executed idea. The comprehensive experiments demonstrate clear performance improvements.
The main reason for not assigning a higher score is the use of image-level split for the training and testing on the dataset.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Harnessing the Full Potential of RRAMs through Scalable and Distributed In-Memory Computing with Integrated Error Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13298v1)
- **Authors**: Huynh Q. N. Vo, Md Tawsif Rahman Chowdhury, Paritosh Ramanan, Murat Yildirim, Gozde Tutuncuoglu
- **Abstract**: Exponential growth in global computing demand is exacerbated due to the higher-energy requirements of conventional architectures, primarily due to energy-intensive data movement. In-memory computing with Resistive Random Access Memory (RRAM) addresses this by co-integrating memory and processing, but faces significant hurdles related to device-level non-idealities and poor scalability for large computing tasks. Here, we introduce \textbf{MELISO+} (In-\textbf{Me}mory \textbf{Li}near \textbf{So}lver), a full-stack, distributed framework for energy-efficient in-memory computing. MELISO+ proposes a novel two-tier error correction mechanism to mitigate device non-idealities and develops a distributed RRAM computing framework to enable matrix computations exceeding dimensions of $65,000 \times 65,000$. This approach reduces first- and second-order arithmetic errors due to device non-idealities by over 90\%, enhances energy efficiency by three to five orders of magnitude, and decreases latency 100-fold. Hence, MELISO+ allows lower-precision RRAM devices to outperform high-precision device alternatives in accuracy, energy and latency metrics. By unifying algorithm-hardware co-design with scalable architecture, MELISO+ significantly advances sustainable, high-dimensional computing suitable for applications like large language models and generative AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MELISO+, a full-stack framework for scalable and distributed in-memory computing using RRAMs. MELISO+ addresses two major limitations hindering the widespread adoption of RRAM-based computing: device non-idealities (variability leading to errors) and scalability limitations. It proposes a novel two-tier error correction mechanism to mitigate device non-idealities and a distributed RRAM computing framework to enable matrix computations exceeding the size of single RRAM arrays. The authors show that MELISO+ reduces arithmetic errors, enhances energy efficiency, and decreases latency compared to conventional approaches. This allows lower-precision RRAM devices to outperform higher-precision alternatives in terms of accuracy, energy, and latency. The framework also includes a full-stack simulation/benchmarking pipeline designed specifically for RRAM-based in-memory computing.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a crucial area in the field of in-memory computing, specifically the challenges associated with RRAM technology. The central premise – that RRAM's potential is limited by variability and scalability – is well-established and widely recognized. The paper claims two main contributions: an error correction scheme and a distributed computing framework.

*   **Novelty of Error Correction:** The proposed two-tier error correction is presented as a significant innovation. However, the paper lacks a detailed theoretical analysis of the error model used and the limitations of the proposed scheme under different noise conditions. Is it truly a fundamentally new approach, or a combination and fine-tuning of existing techniques? While the empirical results are promising, a deeper analysis is needed to fully understand the novelty and generalizability of the approach. Is the error correction truly hardware-aware, or does it make simplifying assumptions that may not hold across diverse RRAM technologies? The algorithmic specifics (implementation details of how "adjustableWriteandVerify" scheme adjusts weights) are insufficient for true peer validation.

*   **Novelty of Distributed Framework:** Distributed computing for larger problems is a natural extension, but the virtualization and address mapping in software are presented as core innovations. What are the overheads associated with this virtualization? The description of the virtualization and chunk partitioning processes, while present, requires more detail to understand their efficiency and scalability, especially considering the analog nature of RRAM computations. It would be beneficial to benchmark the performance of Meliso+ against an existing IMC simulator to determine how closely this simulator models the behavior of real hardware.

*   **Significance and Impact:** The potential impact of MELISO+ is considerable. Overcoming the limitations of variability and scalability could significantly advance the practical adoption of RRAM-based computing. The paper successfully demonstrates improved performance in simulated experiments, showcasing MELISO+'s ability to handle larger matrix operations with increased energy efficiency. The claims of multiple orders of magnitude improvement need careful scrutiny, though, and should be validated on actual RRAM hardware if possible. The detailed benchmarking framework and the methodology of the paper also contributes a useful guide for researchers to evaluate their IMC solutions. The claims about enabling large-scale matrix computations for generative AI are premature without demonstrating those specific AI workloads.

*   **Weaknesses:** The paper could benefit from a more rigorous theoretical grounding of the error correction scheme. More detailed descriptions of implementation choices are necessary, especially in the error correction and hardware abstraction layers. The dependence on simulation alone, without validation on real RRAM hardware, limits the credibility of the performance claims. A comparison against existing RRAM array simulators (e.g., ISAAC) and possibly even fabricated crossbar arrays would be helpful to assess the claims in the paper. Finally, the presentation is sometimes overly enthusiastic, and would benefit from toning down the superlatives in favor of more precise and objective descriptions.

**Justification:**

While the paper addresses a relevant problem with a well-engineered system, the level of genuine innovation and rigor in the validation is somewhat lacking. The paper demonstrates practical potential and integrates existing techniques into a useful and complete framework. However, the lack of deeper analysis of the core algorithms and the reliance on simulation alone (without hardware validation) limits the impact of the work.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 6/10

### GaitCrafter: Diffusion Model for Biometric Preserving Gait Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13300v1)
- **Authors**: Sirshapan Mitra, Yogesh S. Rawat
- **Abstract**: Gait recognition is a valuable biometric task that enables the identification of individuals from a distance based on their walking patterns. However, it remains limited by the lack of large-scale labeled datasets and the difficulty of collecting diverse gait samples for each individual while preserving privacy. To address these challenges, we propose GaitCrafter, a diffusion-based framework for synthesizing realistic gait sequences in the silhouette domain. Unlike prior works that rely on simulated environments or alternative generative models, GaitCrafter trains a video diffusion model from scratch, exclusively on gait silhouette data. Our approach enables the generation of temporally consistent and identity-preserving gait sequences. Moreover, the generation process is controllable-allowing conditioning on various covariates such as clothing, carried objects, and view angle. We show that incorporating synthetic samples generated by GaitCrafter into the gait recognition pipeline leads to improved performance, especially under challenging conditions. Additionally, we introduce a mechanism to generate novel identities-synthetic individuals not present in the original dataset-by interpolating identity embeddings. These novel identities exhibit unique, consistent gait patterns and are useful for training models while maintaining privacy of real subjects. Overall, our work takes an important step toward leveraging diffusion models for high-quality, controllable, and privacy-aware gait data generation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "GaitCrafter: Diffusion Model for Biometric Preserving Gait Synthesis" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GaitCrafter, a novel diffusion-based framework for synthesizing realistic gait sequences in the silhouette domain. The core idea is to generate synthetic gait data to address the limitations of real-world gait datasets, which are often limited in size, diversity, and labeled covariates (e.g., clothing, view angle). GaitCrafter trains a video diffusion model from scratch on gait silhouette data, enabling the generation of temporally consistent and identity-preserving gait sequences. The approach allows for controllable generation based on covariates. The authors demonstrate that incorporating synthetic samples generated by GaitCrafter into a gait recognition pipeline improves performance, particularly under challenging conditions (e.g., limited labeled data).  Furthermore, they introduce a mechanism to generate novel identities by interpolating identity embeddings, providing a privacy-preserving way to expand the identity space for training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in applying a diffusion model directly to silhouette gait data for the specific purpose of data augmentation and controlled generation for gait recognition. While diffusion models are increasingly common in other generative tasks (faces, general video), their application to *gait silhouette synthesis* and the explicit focus on *biometric preservation and controllable covariate generation* is a valuable contribution. Prior gait synthesis works often rely on simulations or GANs, which have their limitations (realism, temporal coherence). GaitCrafter is among the first to leverage the advanced generative capabilities of diffusion models for this task directly on silhouettes. The ability to generate novel identities through embedding interpolation is also a significant contribution, addressing privacy concerns in gait recognition.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial bottleneck in gait recognition: the scarcity of large, diverse, and labeled datasets. By providing a means to generate high-quality synthetic gait data, GaitCrafter can potentially boost the performance of gait recognition systems, especially in challenging scenarios where real data is limited or contains significant covariate variations. The ability to control generation based on clothing, view angle, and carried objects enables the creation of more robust and generalizable gait recognition models. The privacy implications of generating novel identities are also important for wider adoption of gait recognition in sensitive applications. The experiments clearly demonstrate that incorporating GaitCrafter's outputs improves recognition, adding weight to the contributions.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-explained methodology and implementation details.
    *   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with clear metrics.
    *   Demonstration of both visual quality and recognition utility of synthetic data.
    *   Introduction of a novel mechanism for generating synthetic "novel identities."
    *   Public availability of code enhances reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The model is restricted to silhouette images. Although silhouettes offer privacy advantages, extending GaitCrafter to RGB images or other modalities could further improve its impact.
    *   While the experiments demonstrate improved performance, the improvements are relatively modest in some cases. Further research could focus on optimizing the training process, network architecture, or conditioning mechanisms to achieve even greater gains.
    *   The generated sequences are limited to 30 frames, which may not be sufficient for all gait recognition applications. Extending the sequence length or exploring temporal augmentation techniques could be beneficial.
    *   The ablation studies, while thorough, could include an analysis of the computational cost and the impact of various design choices (e.g., pixel space vs latent space).

*   **Potential Influence:** GaitCrafter has the potential to influence the field by:
    *   Encouraging the use of diffusion models for data augmentation in gait recognition and other biometric tasks.
    *   Providing a framework for generating controllable and privacy-preserving gait data.
    *   Facilitating the development of more robust and generalizable gait recognition systems.
    *   Spurring further research into synthesizing novel biometric identities.

**Overall:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of gait recognition. The application of diffusion models to gait silhouette synthesis, combined with the focus on biometric preservation, controllable generation, and novel identity creation, is both novel and significant. While there are some limitations, the strengths of the paper outweigh the weaknesses, and it has the potential to drive further research in this area.

Score: 7.5

*Rationale: The paper demonstrates a good level of novelty by effectively using diffusion models on gait silhouettes for data augmentation and biometric preservation, adding value to the gait recognition community. The controllability and the generation of novel identities are also notable contributions. However, the improvements, although consistent, are not revolutionary, indicating a more incremental than transformative contribution. Additionally, limitations such as the silhouette-only modality and the 30-frame sequence suggest areas for future improvement. Therefore, a score of 7.5 reflects a strong, but not groundbreaking, contribution.*

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Dual-Attention Graph Network for fMRI Data Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13328v1)
- **Authors**: Amirali Arbab, Zeinab Davarani, Mehran Safayani
- **Abstract**: Understanding the complex neural activity dynamics is crucial for the development of the field of neuroscience. Although current functional MRI classification approaches tend to be based on static functional connectivity or cannot capture spatio-temporal relationships comprehensively, we present a new framework that leverages dynamic graph creation and spatiotemporal attention mechanisms for Autism Spectrum Disorder(ASD) diagnosis. The approach used in this research dynamically infers functional brain connectivity in each time interval using transformer-based attention mechanisms, enabling the model to selectively focus on crucial brain regions and time segments. By constructing time-varying graphs that are then processed with Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) and transformers, our method successfully captures both localized interactions and global temporal dependencies. Evaluated on the subset of ABIDE dataset, our model achieves 63.2 accuracy and 60.0 AUC, outperforming static graph-based approaches (e.g., GCN:51.8). This validates the efficacy of joint modeling of dynamic connectivity and spatio-temporal context for fMRI classification. The core novelty arises from (1) attention-driven dynamic graph creation that learns temporal brain region interactions and (2) hierarchical spatio-temporal feature fusion through GCNtransformer fusion.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach for classifying fMRI data to diagnose Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD).  It proposes a dual-attention graph network that dynamically constructs functional brain connectivity graphs at each time interval using transformer-based attention mechanisms. This allows the model to selectively focus on important brain regions and time segments. The time-varying graphs are then processed using Graph Convolutional Networks (GCNs) and transformers to capture both localized interactions and global temporal dependencies. Experiments on a subset of the ABIDE dataset demonstrate that this approach outperforms static graph-based methods like GCNs. The core novelty lies in the attention-driven dynamic graph creation and the hierarchical spatio-temporal feature fusion via GCN-transformer hybrids.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper tackles a significant problem: improving the accuracy of ASD diagnosis using fMRI data.  The limitations of existing approaches, primarily relying on static functional connectivity or failing to capture comprehensive spatio-temporal relationships, are well-articulated. The proposed solution, a dual-attention graph network, attempts to address these limitations by incorporating dynamic graph creation and spatio-temporal attention.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dynamic Connectivity:**  The approach effectively moves away from static connectivity matrices. By dynamically inferring functional connectivity for each time interval, it can better model the temporal evolution of brain activity, which is crucial for neurodevelopmental disorders like ASD.
    *   **Spatio-Temporal Attention:** The combination of spatial and temporal attention mechanisms allows the model to focus on the most relevant brain regions and time segments. This adaptive selection process is likely to improve the model's ability to extract meaningful features.
    *   **Hybrid Architecture:** The GCN-transformer architecture is a promising approach to capture both local and global dependencies in the brain network. GCNs effectively handle the graph structure, while transformers excel in capturing long-range temporal dependencies.
    *   **Demonstrated Improvement:**  The experimental results, although on a subset of the ABIDE dataset, demonstrate a measurable improvement over static GCN approaches. This suggests the effectiveness of the proposed dynamic and attention-based mechanisms.
    *   **Clear novelty:** The novelty is clear in learning temporal brain region interactions through attention-driven dynamic graph creation, as well as the hierarchical spatio-temporal feature fusion through GCN-transformer fusion.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset:**  The evaluation is performed on a subset of the ABIDE dataset.  A more comprehensive evaluation on the entire dataset, or other independent datasets, is needed to validate the robustness and generalizability of the model.
    *   **Modest Performance Gains:** While the model outperforms baselines, the achieved accuracy of 63.2% and AUC of 60% leaves room for improvement. The margin of improvement may not be substantial enough to warrant immediate clinical translation.
    *   **Lack of Biological Interpretability:**  While the paper focuses on improving classification accuracy, it doesn't delve deeply into the biological interpretability of the learned attention weights or dynamic connectivity patterns.  Understanding *why* certain regions and time segments are deemed important could provide valuable insights into the neurobiological mechanisms of ASD.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** It would be beneficial to see the impact of each component of the framework separately. Ablation studies to remove each feature (e.g., spatial attention, temporal attention, GCN, Transformer) would help in understanding their importance.

*   **Significance:**

    The significance of this work lies in its potential to advance fMRI-based ASD diagnosis by incorporating dynamic connectivity and spatio-temporal attention. It contributes to the growing body of research applying deep learning techniques to understand brain networks and improve diagnostic accuracy. The approach could inspire future research into more sophisticated models of dynamic brain connectivity.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, I assign a score of **6.5**.

*   The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach that addresses a critical problem in ASD diagnosis.  The combination of dynamic graph construction, spatio-temporal attention, and GCN-transformer architecture demonstrates a significant effort to model the complex dynamics of brain networks.
*   However, the modest performance gains, limited dataset evaluation, and lack of biological interpretability limit the impact of the study. Further validation and exploration of the model's insights are needed to fully realize its potential. Ablation studies and a more detailed analysis of the learnt representations are necessary for a better understanding of the workings of the model.
*   Overall, it's a valuable contribution with promise, but further work is required to solidify its impact and potential for real-world applications.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Stands to Reason: Investigating the Effect of Reasoning on Idiomaticity Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13365v1)
- **Authors**: Dylan Phelps, Rodrigo Wilkens, Edward Gow-Smith, Thomas Pickard, Maggie Mi, Aline Villavicencio
- **Abstract**: The recent trend towards utilisation of reasoning models has improved the performance of Large Language Models (LLMs) across many tasks which involve logical steps. One linguistic task that could benefit from this framing is idiomaticity detection, as a potentially idiomatic expression must first be understood before it can be disambiguated and serves as a basis for reasoning. In this paper, we explore how reasoning capabilities in LLMs affect idiomaticity detection performance and examine the effect of model size. We evaluate, as open source representative models, the suite of DeepSeek-R1 distillation models ranging from 1.5B to 70B parameters across four idiomaticity detection datasets. We find the effect of reasoning to be smaller and more varied than expected. For smaller models, producing chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning increases performance from Math-tuned intermediate models, but not to the levels of the base models, whereas larger models (14B, 32B, and 70B) show modest improvements. Our in-depth analyses reveal that larger models demonstrate good understanding of idiomaticity, successfully producing accurate definitions of expressions, while smaller models often fail to output the actual meaning. For this reason, we also experiment with providing definitions in the prompts of smaller models, which we show can improve performance in some cases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Stands to Reason: Investigating the Effect of Reasoning on Idiomaticity Detection":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of reasoning capabilities in Large Language Models (LLMs) on their performance in idiomaticity detection. The authors evaluate the DeepSeek-R1 distillation model suite, ranging from 1.5B to 70B parameters, across four idiomaticity detection datasets. They analyze whether chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning improves performance, how well the reasoning outputs reflect understanding of idiomatic expressions, and if definitions generated by larger models can enhance the performance of smaller models.  The study reveals that the impact of reasoning is smaller and more variable than expected.  While larger models show some improvement with CoT, smaller models sometimes perform worse.  A manual analysis indicates that larger models better understand idiomaticity and can generate accurate definitions, but struggle with context and disambiguation. Providing definitions generated by larger models as context for smaller models improves performance in some cases.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing research on LLMs for idiom processing and CoT reasoning. It extends this work by specifically examining the DeepSeek-R1 models and by introducing a detailed error analysis focused on understanding *and* reasoning capabilities. The idea of using model-generated definitions for knowledge distillation is also a relatively novel approach in this context. The novelty, however, is incremental rather than groundbreaking. There is not a completely new setup to model training, but rather a specific application and an error analysis to existing models.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings have implications for how LLMs are applied to tasks requiring nuanced understanding of language. The observation that simply adding CoT reasoning doesn't guarantee improved performance, especially for smaller models, is significant. The detailed analysis highlighting the gap between *understanding* idioms (i.e., defining them) and *reasoning* about their use in context is particularly insightful and points towards areas for future research. The experiment with providing definition has a potential to be further expanded for many linguistic tasks.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper evaluates multiple model sizes and uses several benchmark datasets for idiomaticity detection.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:** The manual error analysis provides valuable insights into the types of errors models make and why reasoning sometimes fails. The definition of understand as the ability to define an idiomatic expression is very well defended in the paper and helps create solid grounds for the reasoning analysis.
    *   **Practical Experiment:** The experiment with using model-generated definitions as a knowledge source for smaller models demonstrates a practical approach for improving performance.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:**  The study focuses primarily on one specific family of models (DeepSeek-R1).  Generalizability to other LLM architectures is not explicitly demonstrated.
    *   **Modest Improvements:** The performance gains observed with CoT reasoning and knowledge distillation are relatively modest.
    *   **Lack of Specificity in Future Directions:** While the authors highlight the limitations of current reasoning approaches, they do not propose concrete, well-defined alternative reasoning frameworks tailored for idiomaticity processing.

*   **Impact:**  The paper contributes to the growing body of knowledge on the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in handling figurative language. Its focus on the disconnect between understanding and reasoning will likely stimulate further research into more sophisticated approaches to contextual disambiguation. The definition generation as a distillation approach, while not showing very good results in this paper, can be the basis of interesting follow up works, for instance on linguistic tasks were there are not a lot of available data to train on.

**Score:** 6

**Justification:**

The paper provides a valuable and nuanced analysis of the effect of reasoning on idiomaticity detection. The detailed error analysis is particularly strong and provides interesting directions for future research. However, the novelty is incremental, focusing on specific models and datasets. The impact is solid but contained to idiom processing and the broader figurative language understanding area. The paper's strength lies in its careful and critical examination of existing techniques rather than in presenting completely new breakthroughs. The limited scope prevents a higher score, but the thoroughness of the investigation warrants a rating above average.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Applications of Small Language Models in Medical Imaging Classification with a Focus on Prompt Strategies
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13378v1)
- **Authors**: Yiting Wang, Ziwei Wang, Jiachen Zhong, Di Zhu, Weiyi Li
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities in natural language processing and multi-modal understanding. However, their high computational cost, limited accessibility, and data privacy concerns hinder their adoption in resource-constrained healthcare environments. This study investigates the performance of small language models (SLMs) in a medical imaging classification task, comparing different models and prompt designs to identify the optimal combination for accuracy and usability. Using the NIH Chest X-ray dataset, we evaluate multiple SLMs on the task of classifying chest X-ray positions (anteroposterior [AP] vs. posteroanterior [PA]) under three prompt strategies: baseline instruction, incremental summary prompts, and correction-based reflective prompts. Our results show that certain SLMs achieve competitive accuracy with well-crafted prompts, suggesting that prompt engineering can substantially enhance SLM performance in healthcare applications without requiring deep AI expertise from end users.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the application of Small Language Models (SLMs) to medical imaging classification, specifically focusing on chest X-ray position classification (anteroposterior vs. posteroanterior). It compares the performance of several SLMs (LLaMA 2-7B, Mistral 7B, and Gemma 7B) under different prompt engineering strategies: baseline instruction, incremental summary prompts, and correction-based reflective prompts. The study aims to find the optimal combination of model and prompt design to achieve acceptable accuracy and usability in resource-constrained healthcare settings where large language models (LLMs) are not feasible. The results show that careful prompt design can significantly improve SLM performance, with LLaMA 2 and the correction-based reflection prompt proving particularly effective.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** Addresses a real-world problem of limited access to powerful LLMs in healthcare, offering a more accessible alternative with SLMs.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Focus:** Systematically explores the impact of prompt engineering on SLM performance, a crucial aspect given SLMs' sensitivity to prompt formulation.
    *   **Clear Methodology:** The experimental setup is clearly described, including model selection, dataset details, and prompt strategies.
    *   **Resource-Constrained Setting:** The experiments are performed on a consumer-grade GPU, reflecting realistic deployment constraints in many healthcare environments.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to a single classification task (AP vs. PA chest X-ray) and only three SLMs. This makes it difficult to generalize the findings to other medical imaging tasks or different SLM architectures.
    *   **Lack of Clinical Integration:** The models are tested in isolation, without consideration for their integration into clinical workflows, interoperability, or human-AI collaboration. This overlooks crucial practical considerations for real-world deployment.
    *   **Narrow Prompting Techniques:** The study explores only a few prompt engineering techniques, leaving out other promising methods like chain-of-thought reasoning or few-shot in-context learning.
    *   **Incremental novelty:** The approach builds on existing research around prompt engineering and small language models, applying these techniques to a specific medical imaging task. The individual components (SLMs, prompt strategies) are not novel, but the combination and evaluation in this specific context provides some incremental contributions.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The paper incrementally adds to existing knowledge by providing an empirical evaluation of SLMs and prompt engineering specifically in the context of a medical imaging classification task. The focus on resource-constrained environments is valuable.
    *   However, the core ideas of using SLMs and prompt engineering are not novel, and the limited scope of the study restricts its broader applicability. While the work provides practical insights, it is not a groundbreaking contribution.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to guide healthcare practitioners in selecting and deploying SLMs for medical imaging tasks in resource-constrained settings.
    *   The findings on prompt engineering can inform the design of more effective SLM applications in healthcare.
    *   The study highlights the need for further research on integrating SLMs into clinical workflows and exploring more advanced prompt engineering techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a practical and well-executed study on applying SLMs to a medical imaging task, with a focus on prompt engineering. However, the limited scope of the evaluation, the lack of clinical integration, and the incremental nature of the novelty limit its overall impact. The work is valuable for demonstrating the potential of SLMs in resource-constrained environments, but it is not a transformative contribution to the field. Therefore, a score of 6 is appropriate.

**Score: 6**
- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Datarus-R1: An Adaptive Multi-Step Reasoning LLM for Automated Data Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13382v1)
- **Authors**: Ayoub Ben Chaliah, Hela Dellagi
- **Abstract**: We present Datarus-R1-14B, a 14 B-parameter open-weights language model fine-tuned from Qwen 2.5-14B-Instruct to act as a virtual data analyst and graduate-level problem solver. Datarus is trained not on isolated question-answer pairs but on full analytical trajectories including reasoning steps, code execution, error traces, self-corrections, and final conclusions, all captured in a ReAct-style notebook format spanning finance, medicine, numerical analysis, and other quantitative domains. Our training pipeline combines (i) a trajectory-centric synthetic data generator that yielded 144 000 tagged notebook episodes, (ii) a dual-reward framework blending a lightweight tag-based structural signal with a Hierarchical Reward Model (HRM) that scores both single-step soundness and end-to-end coherence, and (iii) a memory-optimized implementation of Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) featuring KV-cache reuse, sequential generation, and reference-model sharding. A cosine curriculum smoothly shifts emphasis from structural fidelity to semantic depth, reducing the format collapse and verbosity that often plague RL-aligned LLMs. A central design choice in Datarus is it dual reasoning interface. In agentic mode the model produces ReAct-tagged steps that invoke Python tools to execute real code; in reflection mode it outputs compact Chain-of-Thought (CoT) traces delimited by <think> and <answer> tags. On demanding postgraduate-level problems, Datarus exhibits an "AHA-moment" pattern: it sketches hypotheses, revises them once or twice, and converges avoiding the circular, token-inflating loops common to contemporary systems. Across standard public benchmarks Datarus surpasses similar size models and even reaches the level of larger reasoning models such as QwQ-32B achieving up to 30% higher accuracy on AIME 2024/2025 and LiveCodeBench while emitting 18-49% fewer tokens per solution.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Datarus-R1: An Adaptive Multi-Step Reasoning LLM for Automated Data Analysis":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Datarus-R1-14B, a 14 billion parameter language model fine-tuned for automated data analysis and complex problem-solving. Datarus is trained on full analytical trajectories mimicking expert workflows, including reasoning steps, code execution, error handling, self-corrections, and conclusions. The training pipeline incorporates a trajectory-centric synthetic data generator, a dual-reward system that combines structural and semantic evaluations using a hierarchical reward model, and a memory-optimized Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) implementation. Datarus offers dual reasoning interfaces: an agentic mode (ReAct) for interactive analysis with code execution and a reflection mode (Chain-of-Thought) for concise explanations. The model exhibits efficient hypothesis refinement and surpasses comparable and even larger models on benchmarks like LiveCodeBench and AIME 2024/2025, while using fewer tokens.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several notable novel contributions:

    *   **Trajectory-centric training:** The focus on training LLMs on complete analytical workflows is a significant departure from conventional question-answer pair training. This approach more realistically captures the iterative nature of data analysis.
    *   **Dual reward framework:** Combining a tag-based structural reward with a hierarchical reward model addresses the challenge of balancing format adherence and semantic accuracy, leading to more coherent and well-structured outputs. The HRM incorporating a preference learning mechanism is a significant advancement.
    *   **Memory-Optimized GRPO:** The GRPO implementation with KV-cache reuse, sequential generation processing, and reference model sharding demonstrates significant engineering effort to make reinforcement learning scale to larger models.
    *   **Dual reasoning interface:** Offering both agentic (ReAct) and reflective (CoT) modes provides versatility for different use cases and caters to varying levels of explainability requirements.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper addresses a critical need for more capable and efficient LLMs for data analysis, a field where existing models often struggle with iterative reasoning and error correction.
    *   The demonstrated performance gains on benchmarks like LiveCodeBench and AIME, coupled with reduced token usage, indicate a significant improvement in both accuracy and cost-effectiveness.
    *   The open release of model weights and an interactive agentic pipeline fosters community engagement and allows for further research and development in this area. The reported accuracy is promising.
    *   The "AHA-moment" pattern exhibited by Datarus, characterized by concise hypothesis refinement, is a crucial step towards creating models that mimic expert human cognition.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper is well-written and clearly articulates the technical details of the proposed approach.
    *   The comprehensive evaluation provides strong evidence for the effectiveness of Datarus-R1-14B.
    *   The release of model weights and the agentic pipeline encourages further research and application of the proposed techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The paper relies heavily on synthetic data generation. While the authors describe a robust pipeline, the fidelity of the synthetic data to real-world analytical challenges is a potential concern. Validation on more diverse and ecologically valid datasets is needed.
    *   While the paper demonstrates superior performance on specific benchmarks, it is important to understand the generalizability of the model to unseen analytical tasks and domains.
    *   The dependence on Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct is a limiting factor as it is closed source. The choice of the base model may affect the broad applicability of Datarus.
    *   The paper has not considered how the findings might be affected by bias, fairness, or privacy. It might be useful to identify how such concerns could be addressed in the future.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly influence the development of more capable and efficient LLMs for data analysis.
    *   The trajectory-centric training paradigm and the dual-reward framework could be adopted by other researchers in the field.
    *   The open release of Datarus-R1-14B and the agentic pipeline could spur further innovation and application in various domains.

**Justification of Score:**

Datarus-R1-14B demonstrates a significant leap forward in the development of LLMs for automated data analysis. The innovations in training methodology, reward system, and model architecture contribute to a more capable, efficient, and versatile system. The open release of the model and pipeline will likely stimulate further research in the field.  The reliance on synthetic datasets and the limited generalizability are valid concerns, but the strengths significantly outweigh the weaknesses. Therefore, it is an important advancement that is well received.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### FLAIR: Feedback Learning for Adaptive Information Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13390v1)
- **Authors**: William Zhang, Yiwen Zhu, Yunlei Lu, Mathieu Demarne, Wenjing Wang, Kai Deng, Nutan Sahoo, Katherine Lin, Miso Cilimdzic, Subru Krishnan
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have driven the adoption of copilots in complex technical scenarios, underscoring the growing need for specialized information retrieval solutions. In this paper, we introduce FLAIR, a lightweight, feedback learning framework that adapts copilot systems' retrieval strategies by integrating domain-specific expert feedback. FLAIR operates in two stages: an offline phase obtains indicators from (1) user feedback and (2) questions synthesized from documentation, storing these indicators in a decentralized manner. An online phase then employs a two-track ranking mechanism to combine raw similarity scores with the collected indicators. This iterative setup refines retrieval performance for any query. Extensive real-world evaluations of FLAIR demonstrate significant performance gains on both previously seen and unseen queries, surpassing state-of-the-art approaches. The system has been successfully integrated into Copilot DECO, serving thousands of users at Microsoft, demonstrating its scalability and effectiveness in operational environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FLAIR: Feedback Learning for Adaptive Information Retrieval":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FLAIR, a framework for improving the retrieval performance of copilot systems using feedback learning.  FLAIR operates in two phases: an offline phase where signal indicators are derived from user feedback and synthesized questions (from documentation) and stored in a decentralized manner; and an online phase where a two-track ranking mechanism combines raw similarity scores with the collected indicators to refine retrieval.  The framework is designed to be lightweight, adaptable, and efficient, addressing the limitations of traditional RAG systems that rely on static embeddings and struggle with domain-specific terminology.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of FLAIR through real-world evaluations within Microsoft's Copilot DECO, showing significant performance gains compared to state-of-the-art approaches.  FLAIR has also been successfully integrated in DECO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The novelty lies in the combination of several aspects:
    *   The two-track ranking system, which prioritizes documents based on feedback indicators while also considering general relevance scores (rrf). This provides a mechanism for adapting to user preferences without completely abandoning the initial retrieval strategy.
    *   The decentralized storage of indicators, which likely aids scalability and responsiveness (although this is not explicitly proven through latency experiments).
    *   The integrated use of both user feedback and synthetic feedback generated from documentation, allowing the system to learn even when explicit user feedback is scarce.

    However, each of these elements individually may not be completely new. For example, the general concept of using feedback to improve retrieval is well-established. Reciprocal Rank Fusion (RRF) is well-established. The novelty comes from the integration of those methods.

*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical impact demonstrated within a real-world copilot system.  The evaluation in Copilot DECO showcases the potential for FLAIR to improve the accuracy and relevance of information retrieval in complex technical domains. This is particularly important given the increasing reliance on copilots in professional settings. The scalability is also a significant advantage.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Real-world evaluation:**  The deployment in Copilot DECO and evaluation using real user queries provide strong evidence of FLAIR's effectiveness. This is a significant strength compared to evaluations based solely on synthetic datasets.
    *   **Adaptability:**  The framework's ability to adapt to user preferences and evolving knowledge bases is a key advantage, addressing the limitations of static retrieval systems.
    *   **Efficiency:** The lightweight design and use of off-the-shelf models contribute to the system's efficiency, making it suitable for real-time or near-real-time applications.
    *   **Scalability:** The decentralized indicator storage likely contributes to scalability, although more details on this aspect would be beneficial.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Ablation Studies:** A more thorough ablation study would strengthen the paper. Specifically, it would be beneficial to isolate the impact of each component (feedback, synthetic questions, two-track ranking, and decentralized storage) to better understand their individual contributions.
    *   **Lack of Latency Metrics:** While the paper mentions low latency as a design goal, it does not include explicit latency measurements. This would be important to validate that the framework is indeed efficient enough for interactive use.
    *   **Similarity metric threshold T:** It is very unclear on how the similarity metric threshold T to consider an indicator relevant is set. The rationale on using this parameter and its impact on different metrics is unclear.
    *   **Lack of comparative evaluation with a fully fine-tuned domain-specific model:** The paper claims to avoid the overhead of fine-tuning. However, to fully showcase the benefits, a comparative evaluation with a carefully fine-tuned domain-specific model would strengthen the claims regarding efficiency and adaptation capabilities.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of adaptive retrieval systems for copilot applications. The framework's practical impact and adaptability could inspire other researchers to explore similar approaches for improving information retrieval in specific technical domains. The integration of synthetic data generation with the human feedback is interesting, and could guide related studies.

**Justification for the Score:**

FLAIR presents a solid contribution to the field of information retrieval, particularly in the context of copilot systems. The real-world evaluation and demonstrated performance gains are significant. However, the lack of detailed ablation studies, latency metrics, and some clarity around key parameters limits the paper's overall impact.

### NovoMolGen: Rethinking Molecular Language Model Pretraining
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v1)
- **Authors**: Kamran Chitsaz, Roshan Balaji, Quentin Fournier, Nirav Pravinbhai Bhatt, Sarath Chandar
- **Abstract**: Designing de-novo molecules with desired property profiles requires efficient exploration of the vast chemical space ranging from $10^{23}$ to $10^{60}$ possible synthesizable candidates. While various deep generative models have been developed to design small molecules using diverse input representations, Molecular Large Language Models (Mol-LLMs) based on string representations have emerged as a scalable approach capable of exploring billions of molecules. However, there remains limited understanding regarding how standard language modeling practices such as textual representations, tokenization strategies, model size, and dataset scale impact molecular generation performance. In this work, we systematically investigate these critical aspects by introducing NovoMolGen, a family of transformer-based foundation models pretrained on 1.5 billion molecules for de-novo molecule generation. Through extensive empirical analyses, we identify a weak correlation between performance metrics measured during pretraining and actual downstream performance, revealing important distinctions between molecular and general NLP training dynamics. NovoMolGen establishes new state-of-the-art results, substantially outperforming prior Mol-LLMs and specialized generative models in both unconstrained and goal-directed molecular generation tasks, thus providing a robust foundation for advancing efficient and effective molecular modeling strategies.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces NovoMolGen, a family of transformer-based molecular language models (Mol-LLMs) pretrained on 1.5 billion molecules. The authors perform a systematic investigation into the impact of various language modeling practices (textual representations, tokenization strategies, model size, and dataset scale) on molecular generation performance. The key findings suggest a weak correlation between pretraining performance metrics (like Fréchet ChemNet Distance - FCD) and downstream performance in tasks such as Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) and molecular docking.  NovoMolGen achieves state-of-the-art results, outperforming existing Mol-LLMs and specialized generative models. The authors also find that pretraining saturates relatively early, and even the earliest checkpoints demonstrate strong downstream performance, suggesting the early learning of essential chemical syntax.  The models, datasets, and code are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper's strength lies in its large-scale systematic study of Mol-LLMs.  Evaluating over 30,000 experiments is commendable and provides valuable empirical data. The discovery that commonly used pretraining metrics such as FCD don't necessarily translate to strong downstream task performance is an important contribution. The finding that pretraining saturates early is also interesting and suggests a need to rethink pretraining strategies. Making the models, datasets and code available enhances reproducibility and contributes to the field's progression. NovoMolGen itself presents a practical and competitive option for molecular generation.

However, several aspects limit the paper's novelty and significance.  While the empirical study is thorough, the architecture (Llama) and training paradigm (autoregressive transformer) are not particularly novel. The main novelty rests on the large dataset and the systematic exploration of hyperparameter space related to molecular representations. While the results indicate the benefits of SMILES with BPE tokenization, the performance differences compared to other representations are not overwhelmingly large. There is a reasonable but not groundbreaking performance improvement over existing methods in PMO and docking. The claim of achieving a *robust* foundation for advancing molecular modeling may be slightly overstated; while the model performs well, the gains are incremental rather than transformational. A deeper analysis of the learned representations or a more insightful theoretical framework to explain the observed phenomena would significantly strengthen the paper. Further, the paper could benefit from exploring more advanced pretraining techniques beyond next-token prediction. Comparing the performance of NovoMolGen with models which employ additional pretraining objectives in addition to text prediction such as contrastive learning, or masked language modeling would add further insight and value to the paper.

The score reflects that this is a strong empirical study with good results but lacking in substantial architectural or theoretical innovations.

### Large Language Models as Visualization Agents for Immersive Binary Reverse Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13413v1)
- **Authors**: Dennis Brown, Samuel Mulder
- **Abstract**: Immersive virtual reality (VR) offers affordances that may reduce cognitive complexity in binary reverse engineering (RE), enabling embodied and external cognition to augment the RE process through enhancing memory, hypothesis testing, and visual organization. In prior work, we applied a cognitive systems engineering approach to identify an initial set of affordances and implemented a VR environment to support RE through spatial persistence and interactivity. In this work, we extend that platform with an integrated large language model (LLM) agent capable of querying binary analysis tools, answering technical questions, and dynamically generating immersive 3D visualizations in alignment with analyst tasks. We describe the system architecture and our evaluation process and results. Our pilot study shows that while LLMs can generate meaningful 3D call graphs (for small programs) that align with design principles, output quality varies widely. This work raises open questions about the potential for LLMs to function as visualization agents, constructing 3D representations that reflect cognitive design principles without explicit training.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the potential of large language models (LLMs) to function as visualization agents in the context of immersive binary reverse engineering (RE). The authors integrated an LLM into their existing VR-based RE tool, CogBRE, to dynamically generate 3D visualizations of program artifacts. The core idea is that LLMs, due to their extensive training on code, documentation, and text, may possess latent knowledge of cognitive and perceptual design principles, enabling them to create useful visualizations even without explicit training. The authors conducted a pilot study evaluating LLM-generated call graphs under varying prompting conditions, using subjective human ratings and objective structural metrics. The results show that LLMs can generate meaningful 3D call graphs, but output quality varies. The paper concludes that while the study offers preliminary indications of the potential of LLMs in this area, further research is needed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core concept of leveraging LLMs to autonomously generate visualizations for RE, specifically within an immersive environment, demonstrates a fair degree of novelty.  While there's existing work on using LLMs for information visualization (generating charts, etc.), applying this specifically to the complex and highly specialized domain of binary reverse engineering, and aiming for cognitively-aligned 3D representations is a clear extension.  Prior work in immersive analytics and software visualization has primarily focused on *manual* design or predefined visual metaphors, so the autonomous generation aspect is a key differentiator. However, the lack of a direct comparative study against traditional visualization approaches hinders a full appreciation of its novelty.

**Significance:** The potential significance of this work lies in its ability to lower the cognitive barrier to entry in RE.  By automating the visualization process, LLMs could help RE analysts more easily grasp complex program structures, reducing cognitive load and potentially speeding up the RE process.  The immersive aspect further enhances this by providing embodied cognition benefits. Furthermore, a secondary contribution is identifying and formalizing the MCP framework which is crucial for interaction between the LLM, tool, and environment.

**Strengths:**

*   **Interesting Concept:** The core idea is innovative and well-motivated by the cognitive demands of RE.
*   **System Implementation:** The authors present a reasonably detailed system architecture for integrating the LLM into their existing VR environment. The Model-Context-Protocol framework for structuring LLM interactions is well-defined.
*   **Evaluation Framework:** The pilot evaluation uses both subjective human ratings and objective structural metrics, which provides a more comprehensive assessment than relying on either alone.
*   **Identifies Limitations:** The paper candidly discusses the limitations of current LLMs, such as context window size and token rate limitations, which makes the work more realistic.
*   **Early-Stage Exploration:** the paper's strength is also derived from explicitly being positioned as early-stage research, setting appropriate expectations for the results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Evaluation:** The evaluation is limited in scope, involving only four raters and two small programs. This reduces the generalizability of the findings. While this is appropriate for an early-stage study, a larger and more rigorous evaluation is required to validate the claims.
*   **Lack of Direct Comparison:** The evaluation lacks a direct comparison against existing RE visualization tools or expert-designed visualizations. The authors chose traditional 2D call graphs as a reference but did not directly measure whether LLM-generated graphs actually improved RE performance compared to these benchmarks.
*   **Subjectivity of Ratings:** Although the rating dimensions are inspired by prominent figures in visualization, subjective ratings are inherently susceptible to bias. A more objective measure of RE performance (e.g., time to complete a specific RE task, accuracy of understanding) would strengthen the evaluation.
*   **Limited Insight into LLM Behavior:** The paper primarily focuses on *evaluating* the LLM output, but doesn't provide a deep dive into *why* the LLM generated certain visualizations or how the prompt engineering influenced its behavior. There are some initial indications of impact of the LLM on the output, but further analysis of the LLM's internal reasoning is warranted.
*   **Limited Practical Usefulness:** At present, the LLM-generated visualizations are likely not directly usable by RE analysts due to their variable quality, long generation times, and the need for human interpretation. As such, this work is more of a proof-of-concept than a deployable solution.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **5** to this paper.

*   **Justification:** The paper presents an interesting and novel concept with a well-defined system implementation and a reasonable evaluation framework. The exploration of LLM-driven visualizations in immersive RE is a potentially valuable contribution to the field.

However, the limited scope of the evaluation, the lack of a direct comparison against existing tools, and the current practical limitations prevent me from assigning a higher score. The work has shown the potential for future applications, but further research is necessary to overcome the issues of variability in quality and generalizability.

The paper's main impact is that it identifies a fascinating area for future research into AI-assisted visualization and sets up a framework to enable these studies in the specialized field of reverse engineering.

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 0/10

### MAVIS: Multi-Objective Alignment via Value-Guided Inference-Time Search
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13415v1)
- **Authors**: Jeremy Carleton, Debajoy Mukherjee, Srinivas Shakkottai, Dileep Kalathil
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed across diverse applications that demand balancing multiple, often conflicting, objectives -- such as helpfulness, harmlessness, or humor. Aligning outputs to user-specific preferences in such multi-objective settings typically requires fine-tuning models for each objective or preference configuration, which is computationally expensive and inflexible. We introduce MAVIS -- Multi-Objective Alignment via Value-Guided Inference-Time Search -- a lightweight inference-time alignment framework that enables dynamic control over LLM behavior without modifying the base model's weights. MAVIS trains a set of small value models, each corresponding to a distinct objective. At inference time, these value models are combined using user-specified weights to produce a tilting function that adjusts the base model's output distribution toward desired trade-offs. The value models are trained using a simple iterative algorithm that ensures monotonic improvement of the KL-regularized policy. We show empirically that MAVIS outperforms baselines that fine-tune per-objective models and combine them post hoc, and even approaches the performance of the idealized setting where models are fine-tuned for a user's exact preferences.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MAVIS paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MAVIS (Multi-Objective Alignment via Value-Guided Inference-Time Search), a novel framework for dynamically controlling the behavior of Large Language Models (LLMs) during inference without requiring computationally expensive fine-tuning. MAVIS addresses the challenge of aligning LLM outputs to user-specified preferences across multiple, potentially conflicting objectives (e.g., helpfulness, harmlessness, humor). It achieves this by training a set of small value models, each corresponding to a specific objective. At inference time, these value models are combined using user-defined weights to create a "tilting function" that modifies the base LLM's output distribution towards the desired trade-offs. The value models are trained iteratively using a simple algorithm that ensures monotonic improvement of the KL-regularized policy. Experimental results demonstrate that MAVIS outperforms baselines that fine-tune per-objective models and combine them post-hoc, and approaches the performance of fine-tuning for the user’s exact preferences, at a significantly lower computational cost. It also demonstrates integration with test-time search strategies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty of MAVIS lies in its ability to achieve dynamic multi-objective alignment at inference time, without fine-tuning the underlying LLM. This is a significant departure from traditional methods like RLHF, which require extensive computational resources for each objective or preference configuration. The iterative training of value models that approximate KL-regularized optimal policies and their subsequent use in inference-time tilting represents a novel approach. This inference-time approach allows for runtime flexibility that traditional methods don't provide, and its Pareto frontier extension also signifies a novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of MAVIS is multi-fold:

    *   **Efficiency:** By eliminating the need for fine-tuning large models for every objective combination, MAVIS significantly reduces computational costs and makes multi-objective alignment more accessible.
    *   **Flexibility:** The inference-time control allows for dynamic adjustments of LLM behavior based on real-time user preferences or application requirements.
    *   **Scalability:** The modular design of MAVIS, with small value models that can be easily trained and combined, promotes scalability and adaptability to new objectives.
    *   **Improved Pareto Frontier:**  The claim that MAVIS can extend the Pareto frontier compared to methods relying on combinations of fine-tuned models is important.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Principled Approach:** MAVIS is grounded in a solid theoretical framework, drawing from KL-regularized Markov Decision Processes and soft policy iteration.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper presents thorough experimental results that demonstrate MAVIS's superiority over existing baselines on publicly available preference datasets.
    *   **Practical Implementation:** The paper emphasizes the simplicity of implementing MAVIS and its minimal overhead, making it attractive for real-world applications.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Reward Models:** MAVIS relies on the availability of accurate reward models for each objective. The quality of these reward models directly impacts the effectiveness of the alignment. The paper does not delve deeply into the challenges of creating robust and reliable reward models.
    *   **Limited Scope of Objectives:** The experiments focus on a limited set of objectives (helpfulness, harmlessness, humor, summarization quality).  The applicability of MAVIS to a broader range of objectives, particularly those that are more complex or subjective, needs further investigation.
    *   **Potential for Unintended Consequences:** Inference-time manipulation of LLM outputs could potentially lead to unintended consequences or biases, especially if the value models are not carefully trained and validated.
    *   **Increased Complexity:** There is added complexity in managing inference-time interactions between the base model and separate value models, introducing latency.

*   **Potential Influence:** MAVIS has the potential to significantly impact the field of LLM alignment by providing a more efficient and flexible alternative to traditional fine-tuning methods. The framework opens new avenues for research on dynamic preference modeling, multi-objective optimization, and inference-time control of generative models. Its scalability and adaptability make it well-suited for real-world deployment in diverse applications.

**Justification of Score:**

While the paper is well-written, theoretically sound, and empirically validated, it also contains limitations that diminish its significance. The reliance on reward models, the limited scope of evaluated objectives, and the increased complexity diminish its impact. It builds off known concepts and algorithms and applies them to a growing problem, which reduces its novelty.
