### "Can You See Me Think?" Grounding LLM Feedback in Keystrokes and Revision Patterns
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13543v1)
- **Authors**: Samra Zafar, Shifa Yousaf, Muhammad Shaheer Minhas
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) increasingly assist in evaluating student writing, researchers have begun questioning whether these models can be cognitively grounded, that is, whether they can attend not just to the final product, but to the process by which it was written. In this study, we explore how incorporating writing process data, specifically keylogs and time-stamped snapshots, affects the quality of LLM-generated feedback. We conduct an ablation study on 52 student essays comparing feedback generated with access to only the final essay (C1) and feedback that also incorporates keylogs and time-stamped snapshots (C2). While rubric scores changed minimally, C2 feedback demonstrated significantly improved structural evaluation and greater process-sensitive justification.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates whether providing Large Language Models (LLMs) with writing process data (keylogs and time-stamped snapshots) improves the quality of the feedback they generate on student essays. The authors conducted an ablation study, comparing feedback generated with access to only the final essay (C1) to feedback generated with access to both the final essay and writing trace data (C2).  While rubric scores didn't dramatically change, the C2 feedback demonstrated improved structural evaluation and process-sensitive justification. The study utilized Gemini Flash for feedback generation, with rigorous qualitative coding and behavior-feedback alignment analysis to demonstrate LLMs can leverage process data to provide more context-aware and cognitively-grounded feedback. This offers a valuable perspective on the direction of LLM-assisted learning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of grounding LLM feedback in writing process data is novel and potentially impactful.  While previous work has explored LLM feedback, this paper directly focuses on leveraging keylogs and snapshots to provide a more nuanced and behaviorally-informed evaluation. However, it's worth noting that the concept of using process data in writing assessment isn't entirely new, but the application with contemporary LLMs makes it a fresh approach.
*   **Significance:**  The potential impact on writing education is considerable. The findings suggest that LLMs can move beyond surface-level feedback and offer insights into the student's writing process, making the feedback more relevant and supportive. The identification of specific revision patterns (e.g., difficulty with introductions, structural reordering) that LLMs can detect is valuable.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The ablation study with detailed rubric scoring and thematic coding demonstrates a strong methodology. The use of paired t-tests provides a statistical basis for comparing the two feedback conditions.
    *   **Transparent Reporting:** The authors openly share their prompts, coding criteria, and sample data, enhancing reproducibility and allowing for critical scrutiny of their findings.
    *   **Behavior-Feedback Alignment:** The effort to verify LLM's comments on revision by checking actual Keylogs and shots is appreciable.
    *   **Clear Implications:** The paper articulates the implications for designing more effective process-aware feedback systems.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scale and Scope:** The sample size (52 essays) is modest and the participants were from a homogenous educational background. The Generalizability to diverse contexts needs testing.
    *   **LLM choice:** Gemini Flash is an older model. Although Gemini is cited for accessibility, the results might vary for new models.
    *   **Metric Limitations:** While rubric scores are quantifiable, they might not fully capture the nuances of the improvements in feedback quality demonstrated by C2. A more comprehensive evaluation might include student perceptions of the feedback, or even longitudinal data on how the feedback affects their writing skills over time.
    *   **Limited Behavior Resolution:** As addressed by the authors, it is important to study wider context, sorts of learners and genres of writing to properly evaluate behavior resolution.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research in writing analytics and LLM-based educational tools. It opens avenues for designing intelligent writing assistants that can understand and respond to the cognitive processes involved in writing.
*   **Areas for improvement:** Future research should explore real-time LLM feedback during the writing process and include diverse learners to improve the impact.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed study that contributes to the understanding of how LLMs can be used to provide more effective writing feedback. The novelty lies in the direct integration of process data and the rigorous analysis employed. The limitations, particularly the scale of the study and the narrow diversity in writing genre, suggest that the findings are still preliminary. This is a solid contribution that could stimulate future research in this important area.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Toward Better EHR Reasoning in LLMs: Reinforcement Learning with Expert Attention Guidance
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13579v1)
- **Authors**: Yue Fang, Yuxin Guo, Jiaran Gao, Hongxin Ding, Xinke Jiang, Weibin Liao, Yongxin Xu, Yinghao Zhu, Zhibang Yang, Liantao Ma, Junfeng Zhao, Yasha Wang
- **Abstract**: Improving large language models (LLMs) for electronic health record (EHR) reasoning is essential for enabling accurate and generalizable clinical predictions. While LLMs excel at medical text understanding, they underperform on EHR-based prediction tasks due to challenges in modeling temporally structured, high-dimensional data. Existing approaches often rely on hybrid paradigms, where LLMs serve merely as frozen prior retrievers while downstream deep learning (DL) models handle prediction, failing to improve the LLM's intrinsic reasoning capacity and inheriting the generalization limitations of DL models. To this end, we propose EAG-RL, a novel two-stage training framework designed to intrinsically enhance LLMs' EHR reasoning ability through expert attention guidance, where expert EHR models refer to task-specific DL models trained on EHR data. Concretely, EAG-RL first constructs high-quality, stepwise reasoning trajectories using expert-guided Monte Carlo Tree Search to effectively initialize the LLM's policy. Then, EAG-RL further optimizes the policy via reinforcement learning by aligning the LLM's attention with clinically salient features identified by expert EHR models. Extensive experiments on two real-world EHR datasets show that EAG-RL improves the intrinsic EHR reasoning ability of LLMs by an average of 14.62%, while also enhancing robustness to feature perturbations and generalization to unseen clinical domains. These results demonstrate the practical potential of EAG-RL for real-world deployment in clinical prediction tasks. Our code have been available at https://github.com/devilran6/EAG-RL.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Toward Better EHR Reasoning in LLMs: Reinforcement Learning with Expert Attention Guidance" (EAG-RL):

**Summary:**

The paper introduces EAG-RL, a two-stage training framework designed to enhance the EHR (Electronic Health Record) reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). Recognizing that LLMs often underperform in EHR-based prediction tasks due to the structured and high-dimensional nature of EHR data, EAG-RL aims to intrinsically improve the LLM's reasoning capacity. The framework comprises two key stages: (1) Expert-Guided Trajectory Distillation, which leverages expert EHR models (traditional DL models trained on EHR data) to generate high-quality, stepwise reasoning trajectories using Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). (2) Attention-Aligned Policy Optimization, which uses reinforcement learning to align the LLM's attention with clinically salient features identified by the expert EHR model, further promoting exploration through entropy-aware adaptive clipping.  Experiments on real-world EHR datasets (MIMIC-IV and TJH) demonstrate that EAG-RL improves the EHR reasoning ability of LLMs, enhances robustness to feature perturbations, and improves generalization to unseen clinical domains.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addresses a significant problem:** EHR reasoning is a crucial area for applying LLMs in healthcare, and the paper directly tackles the LLMs' limitations in handling structured EHR data compared to their strength in natural language.
*   **Novel framework:** The EAG-RL framework is innovative. The idea of using expert EHR models to guide the training of LLMs in a reinforcement learning setup is clever and well-motivated. It is particularly insightful how the expert model's attention mechanism is used as an auxiliary reward signal.
*   **Technically sound:** The individual components of the framework (expert-guided MCTS, attention-aligned reward modeling, entropy-aware adaptive up-clipping) are well-designed and justified. The use of existing RL algorithms like GRPO and DAPO is also reasonable.
*   **Strong empirical results:** The experiments demonstrate a substantial improvement in performance compared to several baselines, including prompt-based methods, SFT, and other RL approaches. The ablation studies provide strong evidence for the importance of each component of the EAG-RL framework. The robustness tests and cross-dataset generalization experiments further bolster the claim that EAG-RL leads to more robust and transferable reasoning abilities.
*   **Well-written and clear:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow. The motivations, method descriptions, and experimental setup are clearly explained.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation focuses on only two EHR datasets (MIMIC-IV and TJH). While these are commonly used, the generalization to other datasets or clinical prediction tasks is not fully established.  It would be stronger to evaluate the approach on more external datasets.
*   **Computational cost:**  The reliance on an expert model, MCTS, and RL introduces computational overhead. While the paper describes a lightweight alignment strategy, details on the actual training time and resource requirements compared to the baselines are missing and should be included.  This limits the general applicability of the methodology.
*   **Expert model dependency:** The framework's performance is heavily dependent on the quality of the expert EHR model. The paper does not adequately address how the framework performs if the expert model is noisy, biased, or of low quality. What if the best "expert model" for a specific task is unknown? The paper's robustness is not completely established.
*   **Single expert distillation:** The current form only utilizes the knowledge from a single expert. What are the implications on EAG-RL's performance and clinical reasoning patterns if multiple expert models disagree on clinically salient features?

**Novelty and Significance:**

The novelty lies in the integrated approach of using expert EHR models to guide the training of LLMs for intrinsic EHR reasoning. Previous methods have primarily used LLMs as prior retrievers or relied on handcrafted prompts. EAG-RL is a step towards a more unified framework where LLMs can directly perform EHR reasoning. The significance is that it offers a potential pathway to leverage the generalization capabilities of LLMs for tasks where structured EHR data is crucial.

**Score:** 7.5/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a well-motivated and technically sound framework with strong empirical results, addressing an important problem. The use of expert EHR models to guide LLM training is a novel and significant contribution. However, the reliance on a single expert model, the limited scope of the evaluation (datasets, model sizes), and the lack of detailed computational cost analysis prevent it from achieving a higher score. While promising, further research is required to validate the generalizability, robustness, and practical applicability of EAG-RL in real-world clinical settings. The practical impact and cost-benefit analysis should be examined as well.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Comparative Study of Decoding Strategies in Medical Text Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13580v1)
- **Authors**: Oriana Presacan, Alireza Nik, Vajira Thambawita, Bogdan Ionescu, Michael Riegler
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) rely on various decoding strategies to generate text, and these choices can significantly affect output quality. In healthcare, where accuracy is critical, the impact of decoding strategies remains underexplored. We investigate this effect in five open-ended medical tasks, including translation, summarization, question answering, dialogue, and image captioning, evaluating 11 decoding strategies with medically specialized and general-purpose LLMs of different sizes. Our results show that deterministic strategies generally outperform stochastic ones: beam search achieves the highest scores, while {\eta} and top-k sampling perform worst. Slower decoding methods tend to yield better quality. Larger models achieve higher scores overall but have longer inference times and are no more robust to decoding. Surprisingly, while medical LLMs outperform general ones in two of the five tasks, statistical analysis shows no overall performance advantage and reveals greater sensitivity to decoding choice. We further compare multiple evaluation metrics and find that correlations vary by task, with MAUVE showing weak agreement with BERTScore and ROUGE, as well as greater sensitivity to the decoding strategy. These results highlight the need for careful selection of decoding methods in medical applications, as their influence can sometimes exceed that of model choice.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comparative study of various decoding strategies used with Large Language Models (LLMs) in the medical domain. It investigates the impact of 11 different decoding methods across five open-ended medical tasks: translation, summarization, question answering, dialogue, and image captioning. The study evaluates both general-purpose and medically-specialized LLMs of different sizes, analyzing their performance, sensitivity to decoding strategy choice, and inference time. The authors analyze metric stability using ROUGE, BERTScore, BLEU and MAUVE. The results indicate that deterministic decoding strategies (like beam search) tend to outperform stochastic ones, but the optimal choice is task-dependent. Interestingly, medical LLMs are found to be *more* sensitive to the choice of decoding strategy than general-purpose LLMs. The paper argues that this emphasizes the importance of carefully selecting decoding methods for medical applications, as the influence of the decoding choice can sometimes exceed that of the model choice itself. It also includes a comparison between metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic investigation of decoding strategies *specifically within the medical domain*. While decoding strategies have been extensively studied in general NLP, their nuanced impact on medical text generation, where accuracy and reliability are paramount, is relatively underexplored. The finding that medical LLMs are *more* sensitive to decoding strategy compared to general-purpose LLMs is a novel and important contribution. The comparison of different metrics for the tasks studied is also useful.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Practical Guidance:** It offers practical guidance for practitioners deploying LLMs in healthcare, helping them choose appropriate decoding strategies based on the specific task and model.
    *   **Highlighting Pitfalls:** It highlights the potential pitfalls of blindly applying generic decoding strategies to medical LLMs, demonstrating the need for careful tuning and evaluation.
    *   **Insights into LLM Behavior:** It provides valuable insights into the behavior of medical LLMs, revealing their increased sensitivity to decoding strategies, potentially linked to their specialized fine-tuning or smaller pre-training datasets.
    *   **Impact on Future Research:** By highlighting the importance of decoding strategies, the paper may influence future research in medical NLP, prompting more attention to this often-overlooked aspect of LLM deployment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Approach:** The study employs a systematic and well-defined methodology, comparing multiple decoding strategies across different tasks and models.
    *   **Empirical Evidence:** The paper provides strong empirical evidence to support its claims, including statistical analyses.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The findings have direct practical relevance for healthcare applications.
    *   **Clear and Concise Writing:** The paper is clearly written and well-organized, making it easy to understand.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The study is based on a relatively small dataset of 100 samples per task. While justified by resource constraints, this limits the generalizability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Scope of Models:** The study focuses on open-source models with a maximum size of 14 billion parameters. It doesn't explore the performance of proprietary models like GPT-4 or Gemini, which may exhibit different behavior.
    *   **Metric Limitations:** The reliance on automatic metrics has known limitations, especially for assessing nuanced qualities like factual correctness and user satisfaction. Human evaluation would strengthen the findings.
    *   **Specific Tasks:** The experiments are limited to a finite number of tasks. It would be beneficial to experiment with other tasks.

*   **Justification:** The study's novelty in targeting medical LLMs and its practical implications for healthcare deployment make it a valuable contribution. The clear methodology and empirical evidence are strengths. However, the dataset size limitations and model scope prevent the results from being definitive. The metric analysis contributes to the field, but also leaves room for improvement through human verification. The increased sensitivity of medical LLMs to decoding techniques is interesting and opens the door for further research.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper offers a solid contribution to the field by addressing a critical but often overlooked aspect of LLM deployment in healthcare. The finding regarding the sensitivity of medical LLMs to decoding strategies is novel and deserves attention. However, the limitations in dataset size and model scope prevent a higher score. Future work should aim to validate the findings on larger datasets, with more diverse models (including proprietary ones), and incorporate human evaluation to complement the automatic metrics.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Temporal-Conditional Referring Video Object Segmentation with Noise-Free Text-to-Video Diffusion Model
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13584v1)
- **Authors**: Ruixin Zhang, Jiaqing Fan, Yifan Liao, Qian Qiao, Fanzhang Li
- **Abstract**: Referring Video Object Segmentation (RVOS) aims to segment specific objects in a video according to textual descriptions. We observe that recent RVOS approaches often place excessive emphasis on feature extraction and temporal modeling, while relatively neglecting the design of the segmentation head. In fact, there remains considerable room for improvement in segmentation head design. To address this, we propose a Temporal-Conditional Referring Video Object Segmentation model, which innovatively integrates existing segmentation methods to effectively enhance boundary segmentation capability. Furthermore, our model leverages a text-to-video diffusion model for feature extraction. On top of this, we remove the traditional noise prediction module to avoid the randomness of noise from degrading segmentation accuracy, thereby simplifying the model while improving performance. Finally, to overcome the limited feature extraction capability of the VAE, we design a Temporal Context Mask Refinement (TCMR) module, which significantly improves segmentation quality without introducing complex designs. We evaluate our method on four public RVOS benchmarks, where it consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a new approach to Referring Video Object Segmentation (RVOS). The method, termed Temporal-Conditional Referring Video Object Segmentation, integrates existing segmentation methods to improve boundary segmentation, utilizes a text-to-video diffusion model for feature extraction, and eliminates the traditional noise prediction module to improve segmentation accuracy by simplifying the model. It further introduces a Temporal Context Mask Refinement (TCMR) module to refine segmentation quality.  The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on four public RVOS benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper proposes some innovations, primarily focusing on the segmentation head and diffusion model integration.

*   **Hybrid CondDot (HCD) Segmentation Head:**  This is presented as a key contribution. The idea of combining dynamic weight adaptation (CondInst) with the semantic richness of dot-product-based mask generation is reasonable. The performance improvement over existing heads like CondInst suggests some novelty. However, the combination is not fundamentally groundbreaking; it's an engineering improvement.
*   **Noise-Free Diffusion Model:** The ablation study's finding that removing the noise prediction module from the diffusion model improves performance for RVOS is a somewhat surprising, yet interesting observation. Conventional wisdom suggests noise is essential for generative tasks. Identifying that its removal benefits *segmentation*, a discriminative task, is a useful insight. However, this approach might have limited utility beyond segmentation.
*   **Temporal Context Mask Refinement (TCMR):** Replacing the VAE with a TCMR module to better model temporal dependencies is sensible. VAEs are known to struggle with temporal coherence. While this modification addresses a known problem, TCMR's specific implementation and effectiveness compared to other temporal modeling techniques are not deeply explored.

**Significance:** The paper achieves state-of-the-art (SOTA) results on standard RVOS benchmarks. This is significant, as it validates the effectiveness of the proposed techniques. However, there are some caveats:

*   **Incremental Improvement:** While achieving SOTA, the margin of improvement over previous methods on some datasets (e.g., Ref-DAVIS17) is relatively small. This suggests that the field is approaching a point of diminishing returns, and future breakthroughs might require more radical architectural changes.
*   **Benchmark Dependency:** SOTA performance is always benchmark-dependent.  The long-term significance hinges on whether the improvements generalize to new datasets and more challenging scenarios.
*   **Practical Impact:** The complexity of the architecture and reliance on pre-trained diffusion models might limit its practical adoption, especially if computational resources are constrained.

**Strengths:**

*   Clear problem statement and motivation.
*   Well-organized and technically sound.
*   Comprehensive experimental evaluation with ablation studies.
*   Achieves state-of-the-art results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The individual components (HCD, TCMR) are not entirely novel in isolation. Their combined effect is the primary contribution.
*   Limited analysis of the limitations and failure cases of the method.
*   The novelty in terms of foundational new concepts is limited to the idea about noise. It's more of an engineering optimization of existing techniques.
*   The performance improvement in the diffusion model seems like an engineering decision more than a contribution.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a solid contribution to the RVOS field by achieving state-of-the-art results through a combination of clever engineering and insightful analysis. The realization that noise removal boosts diffusion-based segmentation is interesting. However, the individual components of the architecture are not radically new, and the long-term impact of the work will depend on its generalizability and practical applicability.

- **Score**: 7/10

### PersonaVlog: Personalized Multimodal Vlog Generation with Multi-Agent Collaboration and Iterative Self-Correction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13602v1)
- **Authors**: Xiaolu Hou, Bing Ma, Jiaxiang Cheng, Xuhua Ren, Kai Yu, Wenyue Li, Tianxiang Zheng, Qinglin Lu
- **Abstract**: With the growing demand for short videos and personalized content, automated Video Log (Vlog) generation has become a key direction in multimodal content creation. Existing methods mostly rely on predefined scripts, lacking dynamism and personal expression. Therefore, there is an urgent need for an automated Vlog generation approach that enables effective multimodal collaboration and high personalization. To this end, we propose PersonaVlog, an automated multimodal stylized Vlog generation framework that can produce personalized Vlogs featuring videos, background music, and inner monologue speech based on a given theme and reference image. Specifically, we propose a multi-agent collaboration framework based on Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). This framework efficiently generates high-quality prompts for multimodal content creation based on user input, thereby improving the efficiency and creativity of the process. In addition, we incorporate a feedback and rollback mechanism that leverages MLLMs to evaluate and provide feedback on generated results, thereby enabling iterative self-correction of multimodal content. We also propose ThemeVlogEval, a theme-based automated benchmarking framework that provides standardized metrics and datasets for fair evaluation. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate the significant advantages and potential of our framework over several baselines, highlighting its effectiveness and great potential for generating automated Vlogs.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the "PersonaVlog: Personalized Multimodal Vlog Generation with Multi-Agent Collaboration and Iterative Self-Correction" paper based on the information provided.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PersonaVlog, a framework for automated generation of personalized and stylized Video Logs (Vlogs). The framework takes as input a theme and a reference image of a character. It uses a Multi-Agent Collaborative Framework (MACF) based on multimodal large language models (MLLMs) to generate stories, storyboards, video descriptions, character monologues, and music descriptions. A key component is a Feedback and Rollback Mechanism (FRM) that leverages MLLMs to evaluate and correct the generated content iteratively. Finally, the paper proposes ThemeVlogEval, a theme-based automated benchmarking framework for evaluation. The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their approach through experiments comparing it to several baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper demonstrates moderate novelty. While multi-agent systems and iterative refinement methods have been explored in video generation, the application to personalized Vlog generation, with a focus on theme and character personalization, is a reasonable step forward. The novelty lies in the specific combination of components, particularly the integration of FRM for multimodal content, and the tailored evaluation framework, ThemeVlogEval.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically relevant problem: automating Vlog creation to lower the barrier to content creation. If successful, such a framework could have a significant impact on social media and content creation platforms. The proposed method provides personalized content, which is important in the context of Vlogs.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The use of a multi-agent collaborative framework allows for a structured generation process, breaking down the complex task into manageable sub-tasks.
    *   The Feedback and Rollback Mechanism is crucial for improving the quality and consistency of the generated content, addressing a common issue in generative models.
    *   The introduction of ThemeVlogEval provides a standardized benchmark for evaluating Vlog generation models, facilitating future research and comparison.
    * The use of readily available resources and LLMs makes the solution deployable.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper's reliance on MLLMs makes it susceptible to the limitations inherent in these models, such as semantic hallucinations and biases. The paper needs to address these potential issues.
    *   The FRM could introduce biases based on the evaluation criteria. If the MLLM-based reviewers have limitations, the refined content may only improve within a limited scope, potentially overfitting to the reviewer's biases. The paper would benefit from discussing the limitations of the evaluation criteria.
    *   The degree of personalization achievable might be limited by the reference image and theme. The framework's ability to capture the nuanced personality of a real person remains unclear.
    *   The success of the video largely hinges on the image-to-video generation. If this stage is weak, the entire process will be suboptimal.
    *   The qualitative and quantitative results should be evaluated based on human raters rather than relying only on the MLLMs to provide a robust assessment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could inspire further research in personalized content generation, particularly for Vlog creation. The multi-agent collaboration and iterative refinement strategies could be adopted and adapted for other creative tasks. ThemeVlogEval could become a valuable resource for the community, promoting standardized evaluation and comparison.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a practically motivated approach to automated Vlog generation. The combination of multi-agent collaboration, iterative refinement, and a dedicated evaluation framework is a positive contribution. However, the reliance on MLLMs, with their inherent limitations, the limited evidence of human evaluation, and the somewhat incremental nature of the novelty, necessitate a slightly conservative score. The paper is a good engineering effort but lacks the "breakthrough" elements that would justify a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Score**: 7/10

### Who Gets the Mic? Investigating Gender Bias in the Speaker Assignment of a Speech-LLM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13603v1)
- **Authors**: Dariia Puhach, Amir H. Payberah, Éva Székely
- **Abstract**: Similar to text-based Large Language Models (LLMs), Speech-LLMs exhibit emergent abilities and context awareness. However, whether these similarities extend to gender bias remains an open question. This study proposes a methodology leveraging speaker assignment as an analytic tool for bias investigation. Unlike text-based models, which encode gendered associations implicitly, Speech-LLMs must produce a gendered voice, making speaker selection an explicit bias cue. We evaluate Bark, a Text-to-Speech (TTS) model, analyzing its default speaker assignments for textual prompts. If Bark's speaker selection systematically aligns with gendered associations, it may reveal patterns in its training data or model design. To test this, we construct two datasets: (i) Professions, containing gender-stereotyped occupations, and (ii) Gender-Colored Words, featuring gendered connotations. While Bark does not exhibit systematic bias, it demonstrates gender awareness and has some gender inclinations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates gender bias in the speaker assignment of the Speech-LLM Bark.  The authors hypothesize that biases present in the training data or model design might manifest as systematic gender preferences in speaker selection. To test this, they create two datasets: Professions (sentences containing stereotypically gendered occupations) and Gender-Colored Words (sentences with gender-associated words).  They evaluate Bark's default speaker assignments across these datasets, using a speaker gender recognition model to classify the generated audio.  They find that Bark doesn't exhibit strong systematic bias, demonstrating diversity in speaker assignments. However, they identify some specific words/professions that consistently lead to gendered outputs and demonstrate that Bark infers gender information at one of its layers. They also analyze how speaker prompts interact with textual cues in influencing gender assignment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its approach of using speaker assignment as a *direct* method for analyzing gender bias in a Speech-LLM. Unlike text-based LLM bias evaluations that often rely on indirect inferences (e.g., pronoun resolution), this method leverages the inherent gender signal produced in speech synthesis. The creation of the Professions and Gender-Colored Words datasets specifically tailored for this analysis is also a valuable contribution. The investigation into how the speaker prompt and the text input (through the semantic layer) interact in influencing gender is another novel element.

*   **Significance:** While the finding that Bark *doesn't* exhibit strong systematic bias might seem like a negative result, it's still significant. It indicates that the model isn't simply reproducing blatant stereotypes. However, the identification of specific words and professions that *do* exhibit gendered preferences suggests areas for further investigation and potential mitigation strategies.  The methodological approach could be a template for evaluating bias in other Speech-LLMs. The paper also highlights the importance of carefully considering gender representation in speech synthesis models, given their potential to reinforce or challenge societal norms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Methodology:** The experimental setup is well-defined, and the use of a speaker gender recognition model provides a quantitative way to assess speaker assignments.
    *   **Controlled Datasets:** The creation of the Professions and Gender-Colored Words datasets allows for targeted investigation of potential bias.
    *   **Analysis of Interaction Effects:**  The exploration of how text input and speaker prompts interact is insightful.
    *   **Code and Data Availability:**  The authors made their code and datasets publicly available, promoting reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Binary Gender Framework:** The reliance on a binary gender classification is a significant limitation. It fails to account for non-binary gender identities and simplifies the complexity of gender.  The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **English-Specific:**  The study focuses exclusively on English, limiting the generalizability of the findings to other languages.  Bark is multilingual, and gender bias could manifest differently across languages.
    *   **Limited Scope of Bias:**  The study focuses primarily on association bias (linking gender with professions/words). Other forms of bias (e.g., representational bias) are not explored.
    *   **Bark-Specific:** The analysis is specific to Bark. While the methodology can be extended, the conclusions may not directly translate to other Speech-LLMs with different architectures and training data.
    *   **Manual Analysis:** While the majority of work is automated there is still some manual verification, which could introduce bias in the testing of low-quality generations.
    *   **Small number of English Speaker Prompts:** The models used were limited to only 10 English speaker prompts, 9 male and 1 female. This skewed data may have led to inaccurate results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's influence lies primarily in its methodological contribution.  It provides a framework for analyzing gender bias in Speech-LLMs that other researchers can adapt and extend. The identification of specific words/professions exhibiting gendered preferences could inform efforts to debias training data or model architectures.

*   **Rigour:** The study uses statistical methods to analyze the results and performs significance testing.

* **Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-executed methodology for investigating gender bias in Speech-LLMs. While the findings regarding Bark's lack of *strong* systematic bias are somewhat expected, the insights into the specific words/professions exhibiting bias and the interaction effects of text and prompts are valuable. The limitations (binary gender, English-specific, Bark-specific) temper the significance of the findings. The methodological contribution and code availability are strengths. The small number of English Speaker Prompts and manual anaysis reduce the rigour.

### DiffIER: Optimizing Diffusion Models with Iterative Error Reduction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13628v1)
- **Authors**: Ao Chen, Lihe Ding, Tianfan Xue
- **Abstract**: Diffusion models have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in generating high-quality samples and enhancing performance across diverse domains through Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG). However, the quality of generated samples is highly sensitive to the selection of the guidance weight. In this work, we identify a critical ``training-inference gap'' and we argue that it is the presence of this gap that undermines the performance of conditional generation and renders outputs highly sensitive to the guidance weight. We quantify this gap by measuring the accumulated error during the inference stage and establish a correlation between the selection of guidance weight and minimizing this gap. Furthermore, to mitigate this gap, we propose DiffIER, an optimization-based method for high-quality generation. We demonstrate that the accumulated error can be effectively reduced by an iterative error minimization at each step during inference. By introducing this novel plug-and-play optimization framework, we enable the optimization of errors at every single inference step and enhance generation quality. Empirical results demonstrate that our proposed method outperforms baseline approaches in conditional generation tasks. Furthermore, the method achieves consistent success in text-to-image generation, image super-resolution, and text-to-speech generation, underscoring its versatility and potential for broad applications in future research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "DiffIER: Optimizing Diffusion Models with Iterative Error Reduction" addresses the sensitivity of diffusion models to the guidance weight in conditional generation tasks. The authors identify a "training-inference gap" as a key factor contributing to this sensitivity. They quantify this gap by measuring accumulated error during inference and propose DiffIER, an optimization-based method, to reduce this error iteratively at each step of the inference process. DiffIER is presented as a training-free, plug-and-play method applicable to various diffusion-based tasks, including text-to-image generation, image super-resolution, and text-to-speech. The authors demonstrate improved performance compared to baseline methods across these tasks, highlighting the versatility and potential of their approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of addressing the training-inference gap in diffusion models is interesting, but the implementation feels incremental.  Prior work has explored various ways to improve diffusion models, and DiffIER essentially performs gradient-based optimization during inference. While the authors frame this within the context of the training-inference gap, the core concept of iterative refinement through optimization is not entirely new. The theoretical connection made between CFG weight and the training-inference gap using error analysis (equation 9) and an optimal weight value is novel and interesting to see.

*   **Significance:** Reducing the dependence on careful guidance weight tuning and improving the stability of diffusion models is a valuable goal. If DiffIER reliably achieves this across multiple tasks, it could have a practical impact. The experiments demonstrate improved performance, however the degree of improvement varies across tasks. Showing consistent gains across a broader range of datasets would strengthen their argument. Furthermore, the reliance on iterative optimization during inference may limit its applicability in latency-sensitive scenarios.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Plug-and-Play Nature:**  The training-free aspect makes DiffIER readily adaptable to existing diffusion pipelines. This is a significant advantage.
    *   **Multi-Task Evaluation:**  Demonstrating improvements in text-to-image, super-resolution, and text-to-speech strengthens the generality claim.
    *   **Error Analysis and CFG connection:** Formulating the problem within a "training-inference gap" framework and connecting it mathematically to the CFG weight selection provides a useful perspective.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Overhead:** The iterative optimization at each step of inference introduces a significant computational burden. The paper needs to explicitly and rigorously quantify this overhead and address its implications.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** The underlying optimization technique lacks substantial innovation. The novelty primarily lies in the problem formulation and application to diffusion models.
    *   **Limited Depth of Analysis:** While the paper identifies the training-inference gap, a deeper analysis of the root causes of this gap within the diffusion model architecture would be beneficial.
    *   **Experimental Scope:** Although the multi-task evaluation is positive, additional datasets and a more comprehensive comparison with state-of-the-art methods within each specific task would further validate DiffIER's effectiveness.

*   **Potential Impact:** If the computational costs can be managed and the improvements are demonstrated more consistently across diverse datasets, DiffIER could become a valuable technique for improving the reliability and performance of diffusion models, especially in scenarios where adaptive guidance weights are needed.

**Justification for Score:**

DiffIER offers a valuable and practical contribution by addressing the sensitivity of diffusion models to guidance weights. The idea of optimizing this gap is insightful, leading to a simple, training-free method. Still, it suffers from considerable additional computation, making the contribution not as substantial as if there were very little additional computation involved.

### CRISP: Persistent Concept Unlearning via Sparse Autoencoders
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13650v1)
- **Authors**: Tomer Ashuach, Dana Arad, Aaron Mueller, Martin Tutek, Yonatan Belinkov
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed in real-world applications, the need to selectively remove unwanted knowledge while preserving model utility has become paramount. Recent work has explored sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to perform precise interventions on monosemantic features. However, most SAE-based methods operate at inference time, which does not create persistent changes in the model's parameters. Such interventions can be bypassed or reversed by malicious actors with parameter access. We introduce CRISP, a parameter-efficient method for persistent concept unlearning using SAEs. CRISP automatically identifies salient SAE features across multiple layers and suppresses their activations. We experiment with two LLMs and show that our method outperforms prior approaches on safety-critical unlearning tasks from the WMDP benchmark, successfully removing harmful knowledge while preserving general and in-domain capabilities. Feature-level analysis reveals that CRISP achieves semantically coherent separation between target and benign concepts, allowing precise suppression of the target features.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "CRISP: Persistent Concept Unlearning via Sparse Autoencoders":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CRISP, a novel parameter-efficient method for persistent concept unlearning in large language models (LLMs).  CRISP leverages sparse autoencoders (SAEs) to identify and suppress salient features related to a target concept in multiple layers of the model.  The method automatically identifies these features by contrasting activations on target and benign corpora. The model is then fine-tuned using LoRA to minimize activation values of salient features on the target corpus while preserving performance on benign data and maintaining text coherence.  Experiments on safety-critical unlearning tasks from the WMDP benchmark demonstrate that CRISP outperforms existing methods, achieving a better trade-off between unlearning efficacy, benign knowledge retention, and fluency. Feature-level analysis confirms that CRISP achieves semantically coherent separation between target and benign concepts, allowing for precise feature suppression.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its combination of SAE-based feature identification with parameter-efficient fine-tuning to achieve persistent concept unlearning. While SAEs have been used for interpretability and steering in LLMs before, their use in **persistent** unlearning, especially with automated feature selection and context-sensitive suppression, appears to be a significant step forward. The contrastive activation analysis to select salient features is also a good contribution. Further, this is more fine-grained than alternatives.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the growing need for robust and reliable methods to remove unwanted knowledge from LLMs, especially in safety-critical applications. The trade-off between unlearning, retention and model fluency is always very tricky. Persistent unlearning approaches that modify the model's parameters are crucial for scenarios where inference-time interventions are insufficient (e.g., open-source model releases). CRISP's parameter-efficient approach reduces the computational cost compared to full fine-tuning while potentially increasing its applicability.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Automated Feature Selection:** Automating the feature selection process reduces the reliance on manual selection or heuristics, potentially making the method more scalable and adaptable.
    *   **Parameter Efficiency:** Using LoRA for fine-tuning significantly reduces the number of trainable parameters, enabling efficient unlearning even for large models.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper presents a thorough evaluation on standard benchmarks, including both quantitative and qualitative analysis. The inclusion of fluency and concept metrics helps to assess the impact of unlearning on model generation quality.
    *   **Feature-level Analysis:** Providing detailed explanations of the learned SAE features increases confidence and understandability.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on Pre-trained SAEs:** The performance of CRISP depends on the quality of the pre-trained SAEs. The paper does not explore the impact of different SAE training strategies or architectures.  If the SAEs are not well-trained or cannot adequately capture the relevant concepts, the unlearning performance may be limited. It is not clear how well this method would transfer to models where suitable SAE's do not already exist.

    *   **Limited Evaluation Domains:** While the focus on safety-critical domains is important, the evaluation is still limited to biosecurity and cybersecurity.  It is not clear how well CRISP would generalize to other types of knowledge or tasks.

    *   **Lack of Theoretical Guarantees:** The paper lacks theoretical guarantees of complete knowledge removal. Like most unlearning methods, CRISP does not guarantee that all traces of the target concept will be eliminated from the model.

    *   **Interpretability Limitations:** While the paper includes a feature-level analysis, the process of interpreting SAE features can still be subjective and challenging, especially for more complex models or concepts. In addition, the reliance on Neuronpedia explanations comes with their own caveats.
    *   **Scaling:** The experiments were performed on the relatively smaller Llama 3.1 8B and Gemma 2 2B. The authors should comment on whether this method will scale to much bigger models.

*   **Potential Influence:** CRISP has the potential to influence future research in concept unlearning by demonstrating the effectiveness of SAEs for targeted and persistent knowledge removal. The approach could inspire new methods that combine interpretability techniques with parameter-efficient fine-tuning strategies.

**Score:** 8/10

**Justification:**

CRISP represents a significant advancement in the field of concept unlearning. The automated feature selection, parameter efficiency, and comprehensive evaluation are strong points. However, the dependence on existing SAEs and the limited evaluation domains slightly detract from the overall impact. The lack of any theoretical guarantees is a limitation that has to be taken into account when assigning a score. Overall, the work is very solid but perhaps more incremental than revolutionary. However, it introduces a well-designed and effective methodology.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Input Time Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13654v1)
- **Authors**: Rapheal Huang, Weilong Guo
- **Abstract**: Current Large Language Models (LLMs) are usually post-trained on large-scale carefully curated datasets (data & training scaling) and doing reasoning in test time (inference time scaling). In this work, we present a new scaling paradigm, Input Time Scaling, to complement previous scaling methods by putting resources on queries (input time). During training and testing, we combine meta-knowledge from LLMs to refine inputs with different strategies. We also find a new phenomenon, training-testing co-design there. We need to apply query strategies during both training and testing. Only applying strategies on training or testing would seriously degrade the performance. We are also surprised to find that seemingly low data quality datasets can gain high performance. Adding irrelevant information to the queries, randomly selecting examples from a minimally filtered dataset, can even perform the best. These findings contradict the widely held inductive bias, "garbage in, garbage out". Curating datasets with seemingly high-quality data can even potentially limit the performance ceiling. In addition, models trained on more data with similar quality (15k VS 1k) perform worse, simple dataset size scaling should also be carefully inspected. The good news is that our findings are compatible with the Less is More phenomenon. A small set of examples is enough to evoke high-level reasoning ability. With experiments on models trained on Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, we are able to reach SOTA performance among 32B models on AIME24(76.7%) and AIME25(76.7%) pass@1. We can further achieve AIME24(76.7%) and AIME25(80%) with a majority vote of three models. Starting from DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, the best result would be 86.7% on AIME24 and 76.7% on AIME25. To facilitate reproducibility and further research, we are working on open-source our datasets, data pipelines, evaluation results, and checkpoints.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a new scaling paradigm for Large Language Models (LLMs) called "Input Time Scaling." This approach focuses on refining input queries during both training and testing by leveraging the meta-cognitive capabilities of the LLMs themselves to generate diverse perspectives (personas). The authors find that strategically varying the input through persona generation (e.g., using similar, dissimilar, or random personas) can significantly boost reasoning abilities, sometimes even exceeding the performance of models trained with much larger datasets and more complex reinforcement learning pipelines.  A key finding is the "training-testing co-design" phenomenon, where specific combinations of input modification strategies during training and testing are crucial for optimal performance.  The paper also challenges the conventional wisdom that higher quality and larger datasets are always better, suggesting that diversity in the training data might be more important. Experiments are performed on Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B models, demonstrating competitive or state-of-the-art results on the AIME24 and AIME25 math reasoning benchmarks with a relatively small training dataset of 1k or 15k examples from OpenThoughts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The "Input Time Scaling" paradigm is a relatively novel contribution. While test-time prompting strategies and data augmentation are not new concepts, the systematic exploration of persona generation strategies during both training and testing, along with the "training-testing co-design" finding, distinguishes this work.  The counter-intuitive finding regarding the importance of diversity over dataset quality also adds to the novelty. However, the specific implementation (persona generation through LLM meta-cognition) might be seen as an extension of existing prompting techniques.

* **Significance:** If the findings hold up under further scrutiny and with different models/datasets, the implications could be significant.  The potential to achieve strong reasoning performance with smaller, less curated datasets and simpler training pipelines would democratize LLM development and reduce the reliance on massive computational resources.  The challenge to the "garbage in, garbage out" paradigm is intriguing and warrants further investigation. However, the paper's significance is somewhat limited by the relatively narrow evaluation scope (primarily math reasoning tasks).

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and Well-Defined Paradigm:** The "Input Time Scaling" concept is clearly articulated.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results on the AIME benchmarks. The SOTA claim is well-supported.
    * **Interesting Counter-Intuitive Findings:** The results challenging the quality and quantity heuristics are thought-provoking.
    * **Emphasis on Reproducibility:** The authors commit to releasing datasets, pipelines, and checkpoints.
    * **Train-test Co-design is a significant highlight:** This is a novel and crucial discovery.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Evaluation Scope:** The primary focus on math reasoning tasks limits the generalizability of the findings. It's unclear whether Input Time Scaling would be as effective in other domains.
    * **Dependence on LLM Meta-Cognition:** The reliance on LLM-generated personas means the approach is inherently tied to the capabilities of existing LLMs and might not be applicable in settings where those resources are unavailable. The quality of those personas is also questionable.
    * **Data Contamination Concerns:**  The potential for data contamination is acknowledged but not fully addressed.  While the authors argue against memorization, a more rigorous analysis (e.g., checking for overlap between the training data, persona generation prompts, and test sets) would be beneficial.
    * **Lack of Theoretical Justification:** There is not a good argument as to why some particular techniques did so well.
    * **Lack of Robustness Check:** The results may be overfitting. There is not a clear analysis about why some strategy combinations performed better than others.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in LLM training and prompting.  If the benefits of Input Time Scaling can be consistently demonstrated across different domains, it could lead to a shift in focus from simply scaling up datasets and models to more sophisticated input manipulation strategies.  The findings regarding the importance of diversity could also prompt a re-evaluation of existing data curation practices.

* **Rigorous Rationale for Score:** The paper presents a novel concept with compelling empirical evidence, challenging long held beliefs. It is also simple and highly reproducible. However, there is not a good theoretical justification for its effectiveness. The reliance on LLMs limits its usability and generalizability. Its narrow application also limits its potential impact to math related problems.

### The Hidden Cost of Readability: How Code Formatting Silently Consumes Your LLM Budget
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13666v1)
- **Authors**: Dangfeng Pan, Zhensu Sun, Cenyuan Zhang, David Lo, Xiaoning Du
- **Abstract**: Source code is usually formatted with elements like indentation and newlines to improve readability for human developers. However, these visual aids do not seem to be beneficial for large language models (LLMs) in the same way since the code is processed as a linear sequence of tokens. Furthermore, these additional tokens can lead to increased computational costs and longer response times for LLMs. If such formatting elements are non-essential to LLMs, we can reduce such costs by removing them from the code. To figure out the role played by formatting elements, we conduct a comprehensive empirical study to evaluate the impact of code formatting on LLM performance and efficiency. Through large-scale experiments on Fill-in-the-Middle Code Completion tasks across four programming languages (Java, Python, C++, C\#) and ten LLMs-including both commercial and open-source models-we systematically analyze token count and performance when formatting elements are removed. Key findings indicate that LLMs can maintain performance across formatted code and unformatted code, achieving an average input token reduction of 24.5\% with negligible output token reductions. This makes code format removal a practical optimization strategy for improving LLM efficiency. Further exploration reveals that both prompting and fine-tuning LLMs can lead to significant reductions (up to 36.1\%) in output code length without compromising correctness. To facilitate practical applications, we develop a bidirectional code transformation tool for format processing, which can be seamlessly integrated into existing LLM inference workflows, ensuring both human readability and LLM efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper "The Hidden Cost of Readability: How Code Formatting Silently Consumes Your LLM Budget" investigates the impact of code formatting (indentation, whitespace, newlines) on the performance and efficiency of large language models (LLMs) in code completion tasks. The authors hypothesize that while formatting enhances readability for humans, it may be redundant or even detrimental to LLMs, leading to increased token counts and computational costs. Through comprehensive experiments involving various LLMs, programming languages, and formatting scenarios, the study finds that LLMs maintain performance with unformatted code, allowing for significant input token reduction without impacting output quality. The authors further explore prompting and fine-tuning techniques to encourage LLMs to generate token-efficient code and develop a bidirectional code transformation tool to seamlessly integrate format processing into LLM workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Empirical Study:** The paper presents a rigorous and well-executed empirical study involving a diverse set of LLMs (both commercial and open-source), programming languages, and formatting configurations. The scale and breadth of the experiments are commendable.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The research has clear and practical implications for improving the efficiency of LLM-based code generation. The findings suggest a straightforward optimization strategy (removing formatting) that can reduce computational costs.
    *   **Bidirectional Tool:** The development of a code transformation tool is a valuable contribution, facilitating the adoption of the proposed optimization in real-world scenarios. The demonstration of AST equivalence is strong.
    *   **Addressing Conflicting Opinions:** The paper effectively addresses the conflicting findings of prior work regarding the role of formatting elements, offering a comprehensive evaluation based on model performance rather than reliance on attention mechanisms.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Task Diversity:** The focus on the "fill-in-the-middle" code completion task may limit the generalizability of the findings to other LLM applications, such as code summarization, translation, or generation from natural language. Would these patterns hold true across all code-related tasks?
    *   **Potential Interactions with More Complex Formatting:** The formatting removal is limited to indentation, whitespace, and newlines. More complex or unusual formatting styles might affect LLM performance differently. Is the tokenization as affected as much as more complex stylistic differences?
    *   **Dependence on Tokenizers:** The token count reductions are dependent on the specific tokenizers used by the LLMs. The paper doesn't deeply explore the implications of using different tokenization schemes. The efficiency gains are only as good as the tokenization used.
    *   **Commercial Model Transparency:** As the authors acknowledge, relying on commercial LLMs introduces limitations due to a lack of transparency in the models' internal mechanisms and training data. The reproducibility of the results may also be a concern.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper provides a novel empirical evaluation demonstrating the cost and efficiency gains of removing standard formatting from the input to LLMs.
    *   It offers practical strategies (prompt engineering, fine-tuning, and code transformation) for optimizing LLM performance in code-related tasks.
    *   It is significant because it quantifies a previously underappreciated cost associated with code readability, directly translating to reduced computational expense for commercial APIs.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could influence the design of LLM-based code generation workflows, encouraging developers and service providers to consider format processing as an optimization strategy.
    *   It can spark further research into more sophisticated code representation techniques that are both efficient for LLMs and human-readable.
    *   It could impact API pricing models by highlighting the importance of token efficiency.

**Score:** 8/10

**Justification:**

The paper presents a strong empirical study with practical implications for LLM-based code generation. The findings are compelling and supported by rigorous experimentation. The development of a code transformation tool is a valuable contribution. However, the limitations in task diversity, potential interactions with more complex formatting, and dependency on tokenizers slightly diminish the overall score. While significant, the optimization of the input format is still relatively minor compared to core advancements in LLM architecture or training methodologies.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 8/10

### Neuro-Symbolic Artificial Intelligence: Towards Improving the Reasoning Abilities of Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13678v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao-Wen Yang, Jie-Jing Shao, Lan-Zhe Guo, Bo-Wen Zhang, Zhi Zhou, Lin-Han Jia, Wang-Zhou Dai, Yu-Feng Li
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promising results across various tasks, yet their reasoning capabilities remain a fundamental challenge. Developing AI systems with strong reasoning capabilities is regarded as a crucial milestone in the pursuit of Artificial General Intelligence (AGI) and has garnered considerable attention from both academia and industry. Various techniques have been explored to enhance the reasoning capabilities of LLMs, with neuro-symbolic approaches being a particularly promising way. This paper comprehensively reviews recent developments in neuro-symbolic approaches for enhancing LLM reasoning. We first present a formalization of reasoning tasks and give a brief introduction to the neurosymbolic learning paradigm. Then, we discuss neuro-symbolic methods for improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs from three perspectives: Symbolic->LLM, LLM->Symbolic, and LLM+Symbolic. Finally, we discuss several key challenges and promising future directions. We have also released a GitHub repository including papers and resources related to this survey: https://github.com/LAMDASZ-ML/Awesome-LLM-Reasoning-with-NeSy.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper draft.

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive survey of neuro-symbolic (NeSy) approaches aimed at improving the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs).  It argues that LLMs, despite their success in many tasks, still struggle with complex reasoning and that NeSy AI, by combining the strengths of symbolic AI (logical reasoning) and neural networks (pattern recognition), offers a promising avenue for advancement. The paper categorizes NeSy methods into three main perspectives: Symbolic → LLM (using symbolic methods to generate or augment training data for LLMs), LLM → Symbolic (using LLMs to invoke external symbolic solvers or tools), and LLM + Symbolic (end-to-end architectures that integrate LLMs and symbolic reasoning components more tightly).  The survey then discusses key challenges and future research directions in this area, covering aspects like multi-modal reasoning, advanced hybrid architectures, and the need for deeper theoretical understanding. The paper highlights specific techniques and recent work within each category, illustrating how symbolic methods can address issues such as data scarcity, reasoning function errors, and representation errors in LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the paper does not introduce a novel algorithm or framework, its primary contribution lies in its systematic organization and categorization of recent research in the emerging field of NeSy for LLM reasoning. This is a useful contribution, as the area is rapidly evolving, and a clear structuring of the landscape helps researchers understand the various approaches and their relationships. However, there are many review papers emerging in the LLM area so the incremental novelty is less significant.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a very relevant and important problem: the limitations of LLMs in reasoning. By focusing on NeSy approaches, it highlights a promising direction for overcoming these limitations and for pushing LLMs toward more robust and reliable reasoning capabilities.  The discussion of specific techniques, along with their strengths and weaknesses, offers valuable insights for researchers working in this field.  The identification of open challenges and future research directions is also beneficial for guiding future work. A limitation is the lack of clear insight. Most of the summary is at the level of the individual papers surveyed.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Categorization:** The three-way categorization (Symbolic → LLM, LLM → Symbolic, LLM + Symbolic) provides a helpful framework for understanding the different types of NeSy approaches.
    *   **Comprehensive Coverage:** The survey covers a substantial number of relevant papers, providing a broad overview of the current state of the field.
    *   **Well-Organized Structure:**  The paper is logically structured, making it easy to follow the different arguments and approaches.
    *   **Identifies Key Challenges:** The discussion of challenges and future directions is valuable for stimulating further research.
    *   **Helpful GitHub Repository:** Providing a GitHub repository with relevant papers and resources adds practical value for readers.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Depth:**  Due to the breadth of the survey, the discussion of individual papers and techniques is necessarily somewhat shallow. It provides a good overview but doesn't delve deeply into the technical details or experimental results of each approach.
    *   **Lack of Critical Analysis:** The paper could benefit from more critical analysis of the surveyed methods.  For example, comparing the relative effectiveness of different approaches on specific types of reasoning tasks, or discussing their limitations in more detail. The paper primarily describes and summarizes, rather than providing deep critical insights.
    *   **Potential for Bias:** As with any survey, there is a risk of selection bias in the choice of papers included.  While the coverage appears broad, it is possible that some important or relevant work may have been overlooked.
    *   **Limited discussion of alternatives:** The paper almost exclusively focuses on NeSy approaches. While this is the intended scope, a brief discussion of alternative methods for improving LLM reasoning (e.g., improved architectures, pre-training strategies) could provide valuable context.
    *   **Missing Evaluation:** The paper does not systematically evaluate the discussed NeSy methods. A table comparing the performance of different approaches across various reasoning benchmarks would enhance the paper's practical value.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to be influential in the field by providing a clear and organized overview of NeSy approaches for LLM reasoning. It could serve as a valuable resource for researchers seeking to understand the current state of the art and identify promising directions for future work. However, its impact will depend on the quality and completeness of the survey, and the extent to which it stimulates further research.

**Justification for the Score:**

Given the above analysis, I am assigning a score of **6.5** to this paper. While the survey is well-written, comprehensive, and addresses a relevant and important problem, it lacks the depth of critical analysis and evaluation necessary for a higher score. While it's a useful compilation, the incremental novelty in the highly active LLM space is not significant enough to warrant a higher rating. The lack of concrete insights and comparative analysis limits its potential impact on the field, although the repository enhances its utility.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### HumanPCR: Probing MLLM Capabilities in Diverse Human-Centric Scenes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13692v1)
- **Authors**: Keliang Li, Hongze Shen, Hao Shi, Ruibing Hou, Hong Chang, Jie Huang, Chenghao Jia, Wen Wang, Yiling Wu, Dongmei Jiang, Shiguang Shan, Xilin Chen
- **Abstract**: The aspiration for artificial general intelligence, fueled by the rapid progress of multimodal models, demands human-comparable performance across diverse environments. We propose HumanPCR, an evaluation suite for probing MLLMs' capacity about human-related visual contexts across three hierarchical levels: Perception, Comprehension, and Reasoning (denoted by Human-P, Human-C, and Human-R, respectively). Human-P and Human-C feature over 6,000 human-verified multiple choice questions, assessing massive tasks of 9 dimensions, including but not limited to essential skills frequently overlooked by existing benchmarks. Human-R offers a challenging manually curated video reasoning test that requires integrating multiple visual evidences, proactively extracting context beyond question cues, and applying human-like expertise. Each question includes human-annotated Chain-of-Thought (CoT) rationales with key visual evidence to support further research. Extensive evaluations on over 30 state-of-the-art models exhibit significant challenges in human-centric visual understanding, particularly in tasks involving detailed space perception, temporal understanding, and mind modeling. Moreover, analysis of Human-R reveals the struggle of models in extracting essential proactive visual evidence from diverse human scenes and their faulty reliance on query-guided retrieval. Even with advanced techniques like scaling visual contexts and test-time thinking yield only limited benefits. We hope HumanPCR and our findings will advance the development, evaluation, and human-centric application of multimodal models.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HumanPCR, a new benchmark for evaluating the human-centric visual understanding capabilities of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). HumanPCR is structured as a hierarchical taxonomy with three levels: Perception (Human-P), Comprehension (Human-C), and Reasoning (Human-R). Human-P and Human-C include multiple-choice questions, while Human-R involves open-ended video reasoning tasks that require integrating multiple visual cues and proactive context extraction.  The paper presents an extensive evaluation of numerous state-of-the-art MLLMs on HumanPCR, revealing significant challenges for existing models, particularly in detailed spatial perception, temporal understanding, and mind modeling.  The analysis highlights the models' struggles with proactive visual evidence extraction and their reliance on query-guided retrieval. The authors suggest that simply scaling visual context or applying test-time thinking techniques provides limited benefits. They position HumanPCR as a valuable resource to advance the development, evaluation, and human-centric application of multimodal models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of the HumanPCR benchmark itself and the specific focus on *human-centric* visual understanding across different levels of cognitive abstraction (Perception, Comprehension, Reasoning). While existing benchmarks address multimodal reasoning, they often focus on specific tasks or lack the depth necessary to evaluate fine-grained human-centric understanding.  HumanPCR's emphasis on proactive evidence extraction in complex human-centric video scenes differentiates it.  The detailed annotation with Chain-of-Thought rationales is another valuable contribution.

* **Significance:**  The significance of the work is substantial because it addresses a crucial area of AI development: enabling MLLMs to understand and interact with the world in a way that is comparable to humans. The benchmark identifies critical shortcomings in current models regarding complex reasoning over visual content related to people and their actions. The benchmark results provide insights into model strengths and weaknesses which the authors hope will spur further research by identifying areas for improvement and guiding future development directions for multimodal models, which may in turn enable more sophisticated capabilities in areas such as robotics, assistive technologies, and human-computer interaction. The detailed analysis of model failures, particularly related to proactive evidence gathering, offers valuable directions for future research.

* **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** HumanPCR is a well-structured, multi-level benchmark covering a wide range of human-centric tasks.
    *   **Detailed Analysis:**  The paper presents a thorough evaluation and analysis of MLLM performance, identifying key challenges and limitations.
    *   **Human-Centric Focus:**  The benchmark's emphasis on human-centric visual understanding is timely and relevant.
    *   **Chain-of-Thought Annotations:** The inclusion of human-annotated CoT rationales is a valuable asset for future research.
    *   **Clear Communication:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the benchmark, evaluation results, and analysis.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Data Source Limitations:** The paper admits that its perception and comprehension levels rely primarily on academic datasets and therefore may not be suitable for every domain.
    *   **LLM-Based Metrics:** Using LLM-based metrics for reasoning may introduce biases or inaccuracies in the evaluations.
    *   **Complexity:** The complexity of the Human-R tasks, while a strength, may also make it challenging for some models to perform well, potentially masking other abilities.  The sheer volume of factors that could lead to an incorrect answer may confound the conclusions that can be drawn from them.

*   **Potential Influence:**  HumanPCR has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmark in the MLLM community. The insights gained from the evaluation and analysis can guide the development of more robust and human-aware multimodal models. The dataset and annotations will serve as valuable resources for researchers working on visual reasoning, context understanding, and human-computer interaction.

**Justification for Score:**

The HumanPCR benchmark fills a crucial gap in the evaluation of MLLMs by focusing on the complex and challenging area of human-centric visual understanding. Its multi-level structure, comprehensive analysis, and detailed annotations make it a valuable contribution to the field. While the benchmark has some limitations, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The potential impact on future research and the development of more human-aware AI systems is significant.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Generics and Default Reasoning in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13718v1)
- **Authors**: James Ravi Kirkpatrick, Rachel Katharine Sterken
- **Abstract**: This paper evaluates the capabilities of 28 large language models (LLMs) to reason with 20 defeasible reasoning patterns involving generic generalizations (e.g., 'Birds fly', 'Ravens are black') central to non-monotonic logic.
