### RotBench: Evaluating Multimodal Large Language Models on Identifying Image Rotation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13968v1)
- **Authors**: Tianyi Niu, Jaemin Cho, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal
- **Abstract**: We investigate to what extent Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can accurately identify the orientation of input images rotated 0{\deg}, 90{\deg}, 180{\deg}, and 270{\deg}. This task demands robust visual reasoning capabilities to detect rotational cues and contextualize spatial relationships within images, regardless of their orientation. To evaluate MLLMs on these abilities, we introduce RotBench -- a 350-image manually-filtered benchmark comprising lifestyle, portrait, and landscape images. Despite the relatively simple nature of this task, we show that several state-of-the-art open and proprietary MLLMs, including GPT-5, o3, and Gemini-2.5-Pro, do not reliably identify rotation in input images. Providing models with auxiliary information -- including captions, depth maps, and more -- or using chain-of-thought prompting offers only small and inconsistent improvements. Our results indicate that most models are able to reliably identify right-side-up (0{\deg}) images, while certain models are able to identify upside-down (180{\deg}) images. None can reliably distinguish between 90{\deg} and 270{\deg}. Simultaneously showing the image rotated in different orientations leads to moderate performance gains for reasoning models, while a modified setup using voting improves the performance of weaker models. We further show that fine-tuning does not improve models' ability to distinguish 90{\deg} and 270{\deg} rotations, despite substantially improving the identification of 180{\deg} images. Together, these results reveal a significant gap between MLLMs' spatial reasoning capabilities and human perception in identifying rotation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces RotBench, a new benchmark designed to evaluate the ability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to identify the orientation of rotated images (0°, 90°, 180°, and 270°). The benchmark consists of 350 manually filtered images, split into a larger and smaller set. The authors assess the performance of several state-of-the-art MLLMs, including GPT-5 and Gemini, revealing that these models struggle to accurately determine image rotation, particularly distinguishing between 90° and 270° rotations. The paper explores the impact of auxiliary information (captions, depth maps, etc.), chain-of-thought prompting, and fine-tuning, finding limited and inconsistent improvements. The research highlights a significant gap between MLLMs' spatial reasoning capabilities and human perception regarding image rotation. They also perform analyses to understand model biases toward certain angles and their abilities to differentiate clockwise and counter-clockwise rotations. Finally, they explore potential solutions such as rotation grids and a voting mechanism.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper tackles a seemingly simple but fundamentally important aspect of spatial reasoning for MLLMs. Identifying image rotation is a basic skill humans possess effortlessly, and demonstrating the shortcomings of MLLMs in this area is valuable.
*   **Carefully Constructed Benchmark:** The manual filtering process in creating RotBench is a strength. It aims to ensure the images are distinguishable across rotations, minimizing ambiguity. The dataset, while small, is focused and well-curated for the specific task.
*   **Comprehensive Experiments:** The paper explores various approaches, including auxiliary information, chain-of-thought prompting, rotation grids, and fine-tuning.  This thoroughness provides a broad understanding of the challenges and potential avenues for improvement.
*   **Insightful Analysis:** The confusion matrix analysis, examination of clockwise/counter-clockwise bias, and visualization of reasoning traces provide valuable insights into the models' failure modes. Identifying the oscillations in performance between 90° and 270° during fine-tuning is also a notable observation.
*   **Reproducibility:** The authors make their code and data publicly available, which is commendable and facilitates future research in this area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Dataset Size:** While the manual filtering justifies the dataset's size, 350 images are relatively small for evaluating modern MLLMs.  A larger dataset could provide more robust statistical results and reveal subtler performance patterns.  The 50 image ROTBENCH-SMALL seems particularly limited.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** RotBench focuses on a narrow task. It would be useful to explore how performance on RotBench correlates with performance on downstream tasks that require spatial reasoning or rotation invariance. This would better demonstrate the practical implications of the findings.
*   **Lack of a Strong Solution:** While the analysis is thorough, the proposed solutions (auxiliary information, chain-of-thought prompting, rotation grids, voting) provide only incremental or inconsistent improvements. The paper successfully highlights a problem but does not offer a clear path toward resolving it.
*   **Incremental Novelty:** While valuable, the finding that MLLMs struggle with image rotation isn't entirely surprising.  Previous work has shown MLLMs are sensitive to simple image transformations. The primary novelty lies in the specific benchmark and the systematic evaluation of various methods.
*   **Lack of comparison against baseline methods:** The authors compare MLLMs against humans, however, the findings could have been more compelling with a comparison against classical computer vision techniques for solving the same task.
*   **Limited scope:** The study has limited the number of images that were used to fine-tune the MLLM to only 1000, however, a compelling point that could have been explored is the increase of performance with a significant amount of images being used to fine-tune it to resolve some issues regarding the oscillations and performance improvements.

**Significance and Potential Influence:**

The paper makes a useful contribution by:

*   **Raising Awareness:** Drawing attention to the spatial reasoning limitations of MLLMs, even in seemingly simple tasks.
*   **Providing a Benchmark:** Offering a valuable tool (RotBench) for future research and development in this area.
*   **Guiding Future Research:** The identified failure modes and insights (e.g., bias toward 0°, clockwise/counter-clockwise confusion) can inform the design of more robust and spatially aware MLLMs.

Despite the limitations, the paper's systematic investigation and clear articulation of the problem justify a reasonable score.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### ChronoLLM: Customizing Language Models for Physics-Based Simulation Code Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13975v1)
- **Authors**: Jingquan Wang, Andrew Negrut, Harry Zhang, Khailanii Slaton, Shu Wang, Radu Serban, Jinlong Wu, Dan Negrut
- **Abstract**: This contribution is concerned with the following issue: can pretrained large language models (LLMs) be refined and customized to the point where they become virtual assistants helping experts with the effective use of a simulation tool? In this case study, the ``simulation tool'' considered is PyChrono, an open source multi-physics dynamics engine for multibody systems. We present a framework for refining and customizing both open- and closed-source LLMs to harness the power of AI in generating scripts that perform PyChrono virtual experiments. We refine and customize several classes of LLMs through a process that leads to a quantifiable improvement in the quality of the generated PyChrono simulation scripts. These scripts can range from simple single-pendulum simulations to complex virtual experiments involving full vehicles on deformable terrain. While the generated scripts are rarely perfect, they often serve as strong starting points for the user to modify and improve on. Additionally, the LLM can answer specific API questions about the simulator, or recommend modeling approaches. The framework discussed is general and can be applied to lower the entry barrier for simulation tools associated with other application domains.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper "ChronoLLM: Customizing Language Models for Physics-Based Simulation Code Generation," based on the OCR results.

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of pre-trained large language models (LLMs) to generate code for the PyChrono multi-physics dynamics engine. The authors present a framework for refining and customizing both open- and closed-source LLMs to assist users in creating PyChrono simulation scripts. They focus on fine-tuning and prompt engineering techniques to improve the quality and accuracy of the generated code, covering applications from simple pendulum simulations to complex vehicle dynamics on deformable terrain. The paper identifies the challenges users face when setting up PyChrono simulations, discusses various methods to produce domain-specific LLMs, details the creation of training datasets, and presents preliminary results showcasing the performance of their customized ChronoLLMs. The ultimate goal is to lower the entry barrier for simulation tools and assist users in creating digital twins of mechanical systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using LLMs to generate physics simulation code isn't entirely new. However, the paper's novelty lies in its specific focus on the PyChrono engine, its systematic approach to customizing LLMs through fine-tuning and prompt engineering, and its analysis of user challenges within the PyChrono ecosystem. Also, the process described for data generation for fine tuning is non-trivial. The paper also details the various ways to produce the domain-specific LLM with associated pro's and cons. The description is informative and the pro's and cons are properly evaluated.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real problem: the complexity and learning curve associated with physics simulation tools. By demonstrating the potential of LLMs to automate code generation, the authors propose a way to make these tools more accessible to a wider audience. This could have a significant impact on engineering design, virtual prototyping, and research. The study also addresses the need for a domain-specific LLM, due to the limitations of generic LLMs, caused by limited up-to-date information and terminology. Addressing this issue and trying to improve it is valuable.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges users face with PyChrono and the potential benefits of LLM-based assistance.
    *   **Systematic approach:** The framework for refining and customizing LLMs is well-structured and covers various techniques. The overview of existing approaches to creating domain-specific LLMs (fine-tuning, prompt engineering, etc.) is also valuable.
    *   **Careful Data Preparation:** The description of the dataset creation process is detailed and highlights the importance of high-quality training data.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The initial results showing improved performance with fine-tuned models are encouraging and support the paper's claims. The inclusion of two case studies also adds value. The discussion about fine tuning with LORA and the cost of SFT for the models are also valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Results:** While the preliminary results are promising, the performance improvements aren't groundbreaking. There's a need for more comprehensive evaluation with larger and more diverse datasets and tasks. The models used were pre-trained and tested, but were "filled from a provided table" (from OCR output) and were left blank.
    *   **Generalizability:** The paper's focus on PyChrono raises questions about the generalizability of the approach to other physics simulation engines or application domains.
    *   **Practical Considerations:** The paper doesn't fully address the practical challenges of deploying and maintaining customized LLMs for real-world use, such as computational resources, model updates, and user support. The LLMs may be limited for the specific domain, but this is still valuable as the paper describes it as a starting point for the users.
    *   **Lack of discussion on other LLMs**: There is limited discussion on the various LLMs that the LLM can utilize to generate PyChrono.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper can influence researchers to explore LLMs for automating tasks in various engineering domains. Also, the paper serves as a starting point for developers of physics simulation engines to lower the learning curve and increase adoption by automating code generation. It encourages further research on fine-tuning techniques, dataset creation strategies, and the integration of LLMs with other software tools.
    *   "harmful and unpredictable content", "insufficient domain data", "lack of robustness", "scalability and efficiency" - these are important issues of the project that the paper brings to light.
    *    The paper has a lot of references - 107.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign a score of **6.5**.

**Rationale:**

While the paper presents a well-structured framework and a promising approach to using LLMs for PyChrono code generation, it has some limitations in terms of results, generalizability, and practical considerations. The value of the study lies in its systematic approach, detailed dataset creation process, and the recognition that LLMs aren't going to completely solve the problem but rather assist the users. More robust experimental results, a broader scope, and a discussion of implementation issues would have strengthened the paper. The work also brings to light issues with the project with the use of LLMs, such as harmful and unpredictable content that can arise, or the lack of robustness. However, the paper opens up interesting research directions and has the potential to contribute to the field of physics simulation and digital twin development.

### Chunks as Arms: Multi-Armed Bandit-Guided Sampling for Long-Context LLM Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13993v1)
- **Authors**: Shaohua Duan, Xinze Li, Zhenghao Liu, Xiaoyuan Yi, Yukun Yan, Shuo Wang, Yu Gu, Ge Yu, Maosong Sun
- **Abstract**: Long-context modeling is critical for a wide range of real-world tasks, including long-context question answering, summarization, and complex reasoning tasks. Recent studies have explored fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) with synthetic data to enhance their long-context capabilities. However, the effectiveness of such approaches is often limited by the low diversity and factual inconsistencies in the generated data. To address these challenges, we propose LongMab-PO, a novel framework that leverages a Multi-Armed Bandit (MAB) rollout strategy to identify the most informative chunks from the given long context for sampling high-quality and diverse responses and constructing preference data pairs for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training. Specifically, we treat context chunks as arms of MAB, select chunks based on their expected reward scores to input into LLMs to generate responses, and iteratively update these scores based on reward feedback. This exploration and exploitation process enables the model to focus on the most relevant context segments, thereby generating and collecting high-quality and diverse responses. Finally, we collect these generated responses from the rollout process and apply the DPO method to further optimize the LLM. Experimental results show that LongMab-PO significantly improves the diversity and quality of preference data pairs, achieving state-of-the-art performance on long-context reasoning benchmarks. All code and data will be released on https://github.com/NEUIR/LongMab-PO.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LongMab-PO, a novel framework for enhancing the long-context reasoning capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through a Multi-Armed Bandit (MAB) guided approach. It tackles the "lost-in-the-middle" problem by adaptively sampling the most informative context chunks for preference optimization.  The method divides long contexts into chunks, treats each chunk as an "arm" in a MAB, uses the UCB algorithm to select chunk subsets, generates responses using the LLM based on selected chunks, and updates the chunk rewards based on response quality. Finally, the method constructs preference data pairs for Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) training using these responses. Experiments on several long-context QA tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of LongMab-PO, showing improvements in both response quality and diversity compared to existing SFT-based and DPO-based baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a fairly novel approach by combining a MAB rollout strategy with DPO for long-context learning in LLMs.  Using a MAB for chunk selection in this context is a clever idea, and the initialization via "evidence probing" helps to bootstrap the process efficiently.  While chunk-aware methods exist, the adaptive exploration offered by the MAB gives LongMab-PO an edge over static, similarity-based approaches.

*   **Significance:**  Long-context understanding is a crucial area for LLM research. The "lost-in-the-middle" problem is a major challenge. The paper offers a practical solution to improve long-context reasoning. The performance gains demonstrated on standard benchmarks support the approach's potential impact. By improving the quality of training data and the ability to leverage different combinations of multiple context chunks, LongMab-PO appears to be a valuable method.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Adaptive Sampling:**  The MAB-guided sampling is a key strength. It dynamically explores and exploits informative chunk combinations.
    *   **Improved Diversity and Quality:**  The results show that the method leads to higher-quality and more diverse responses, indicating an improved exploration of the context space.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The method shows consistent improvements over several strong baselines in long-context QA tasks.
    *   **Ablation Study:** A detailed ablation study helps to dissect contributions of the various components of the method and justifies the design choices.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Overhead:** The MAB rollout process could be computationally expensive, especially for very long contexts with many chunks and rollout iterations. While experiments using LoRA and Llama Factory are mentioned, the exact details of the hardware setup need to be carefully analyzed to see the cost-effectiveness.
    *   **Limited Evaluation Datasets:** While the paper evaluates on standard benchmarks, evaluating on a broader range of long-context tasks and different types of LLMs could strengthen the results.
    *   **Reliance on Evaluation Metrics:** The reward function used within the MAB is based on metrics like SubEM and F1. While commonly used, they are not perfect proxies for reasoning ability.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of MAB-based approaches is sometimes sensitive to hyperparameter settings, such as the exploration factor (α) in the UCB algorithm and the number of selected chunks (K). There is not sufficient discussion about the impact of alpha.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more effective long-context LLMs. The MAB-guided sampling strategy could be integrated into other DPO-based frameworks. If the computational overhead is manageable, it could become a standard technique in the field.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** I am assigned the score 7 because LongMab-PO is solid with novelty in the use of a MAB to guide DPO training by strategically sampling context chunks, empirically shown to boost the performance of LLMs in long-context tasks, has the ability to improve both the quality and the diversity of the responses. The paper makes a useful contribution, even if the approach has a limitation for scaling up to extremely long context lengths. Further work that addresses the computational cost and explores different reward mechanisms could significantly increase the impact.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Ask Good Questions for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14025v1)
- **Authors**: Qi Wu, Zhongqi Lu
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have significantly improved the performance of dialog systems, yet current approaches often fail to provide accurate guidance of topic due to their inability to discern user confusion in related concepts. To address this, we introduce the Ask-Good-Question (AGQ) framework, which features an improved Concept-Enhanced Item Response Theory (CEIRT) model to better identify users' knowledge levels. Our contributions include applying the CEIRT model along with LLMs to directly generate guiding questions based on the inspiring text, greatly improving information retrieval efficiency during the question & answer process. Through comparisons with other baseline methods, our approach outperforms by significantly enhencing the users' information retrieval experiences.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and a critical evaluation of the research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the Ask-Good-Question (AGQ) framework, designed to improve information retrieval using Large Language Models (LLMs) in complex domains. A key component is the Concept-Enhanced Item Response Theory (CEIRT) model, which dynamically assesses the user's knowledge state across multiple concepts. This assessment then informs the generation of targeted, guiding questions by the LLM. The AGQ framework aims to overcome limitations of current LLM-based dialog systems, which often struggle to identify and address user knowledge gaps effectively.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of AGQ through experiments in the Enhanced Oil Recovery (EOR) domain, comparing it against baseline methods and evaluating its performance using both quantitative metrics and qualitative analysis. The core idea is to actively guide the user's exploration by asking questions that are tailored to what they *don't* know, as opposed to just answering what they *do* ask.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The combination of CEIRT with LLMs for dynamic knowledge assessment and question generation is relatively novel, especially in the context of information retrieval. While IRT models and adaptive testing have been used previously, the paper's application of CEIRT to continuously track and adapt to the user's knowledge state during a dialog, and then leveraging this information for guiding question generation is a distinct contribution. The integration of LLMs in the question generation aspect further enhances the novelty.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial limitation of current LLM-based dialog systems, namely the inability to accurately identify user knowledge gaps and provide effective guidance. By improving information retrieval efficiency and knowledge acquisition, the AGQ framework has the potential to significantly impact the development of more intelligent and helpful dialog systems.  The experimental results, demonstrating superior performance compared to baseline methods and approaching human expert levels, further underscore the significance of the work. The claim that their framework can guide LLMs to provide a 'gold standard' of guiding questions is likely hyperbole, but their claims are grounded in their experiments and demonstrate meaningful guidance.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges associated with knowledge retrieval by LLMs, particularly in identifying and addressing user confusion in complex domains.
    * **Well-defined framework:** The AGQ framework is clearly described, with detailed explanations of its components, including the CEIRT model and the question generation process.
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper presents a thorough evaluation of the AGQ framework, using both quantitative metrics (accuracy, text similarity) and qualitative analysis (human evaluation). The use of multiple LLMs and the inclusion of a human expert baseline strengthens the evaluation.
    * **Solid empirical results:** The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the AGQ framework in improving information retrieval efficiency and knowledge acquisition.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Domain specificity:** The experiments are conducted solely in the EOR domain, which limits the generalizability of the results. While the framework is designed to be adaptable to other domains, further experiments are needed to validate its effectiveness in different contexts.
    * **CEIRT Model Complexity:** The details of how the parameters are updated during the user-LLM interaction are not described in full detail. The implementation of the knowledge state as an embedding layer, while providing flexibility, can make it hard to gain explainability and interpretability. A full mathematical description of the optimization would strengthen the contribution.
    * **Dependency on QA Pairs:** The AGQ framework relies on the existence of a structured knowledge corpus of question-answer pairs associated with concepts. Creating this dataset, as mentioned in the paper, is a manual and time-consuming process. Although the authors attempt to automate this process via LLMs, it may still be required to undergo manual expert checking, hindering its application to some real world scenarios.
    * **Text similarity evaluation:** The use of BLEU and ROUGE for evaluating guiding questions is limited, as it primarily assesses lexical and semantic similarity rather than functional effectiveness.
* **Potential Influence:** If the AGQ framework proves to be widely applicable across different domains, it could become a valuable tool for improving the performance of LLM-based dialog systems and promoting more effective knowledge acquisition. The focus on asking questions that strategically address gaps in knowledge, as opposed to merely answering existing queries, represents a potentially significant shift in how LLMs are used for information retrieval.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**  The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to addressing a significant challenge in LLM-based information retrieval. The AGQ framework has the potential to make a valuable contribution to the field, but the limitations related to domain specificity, the complexity of parameter tuning, and dependency on creating QA pairs reduce its overall impact.  Further validation in diverse domains and more detailed characterization of the model tuning are required to increase the paper's score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Beyond Pass@1: Self-Play with Variational Problem Synthesis Sustains RLVR
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14029v1)
- **Authors**: Xiao Liang, Zhongzhi Li, Yeyun Gong, Yelong Shen, Ying Nian Wu, Zhijiang Guo, Weizhu Chen
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has recently emerged as a key paradigm for post-training Large Language Models (LLMs), particularly for complex reasoning tasks. However, vanilla RLVR training has been shown to improve Pass@1 performance at the expense of policy entropy, leading to reduced generation diversity and limiting the Pass@k performance, which typically represents the upper bound of LLM reasoning capability. In this paper, we systematically analyze the policy's generation diversity from the perspective of training problems and find that augmenting and updating training problems helps mitigate entropy collapse during training. Based on these observations, we propose an online Self-play with Variational problem Synthesis (SvS) strategy for RLVR training, which uses the policy's correct solutions to synthesize variational problems while ensuring their reference answers remain identical to the originals. This self-improving strategy effectively maintains policy entropy during training and substantially improves Pass@k compared with standard RLVR, sustaining prolonged improvements and achieving absolute gains of 18.3% and 22.8% in Pass@32 performance on the competition-level AIME24 and AIME25 benchmarks. Experiments on 12 reasoning benchmarks across varying model sizes from 3B to 32B consistently demonstrate the generalizability and robustness of SvS.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Beyond Pass@1: Self-Play with Variational Problem Synthesis Sustains RLVR" addresses the issue of entropy collapse in Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) training for Large Language Models (LLMs).  RLVR training often improves Pass@1 at the cost of reduced generation diversity, limiting Pass@k performance. The authors systematically analyze policy generation diversity and propose an online Self-play with Variational problem Synthesis (SvS) strategy. SvS uses the policy's correct solutions to synthesize variational problems, maintaining policy entropy and improving Pass@k. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of SvS through experiments on reasoning benchmarks, showing consistent improvements compared to standard RLVR across various model sizes.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel approach to data augmentation in the context of RLVR training for LLMs. The idea of using the model's *own correct solutions* to generate variational problems for further training is intriguing and differentiates this work from other data augmentation techniques that rely on external rephrasing models or human annotation. The self-play aspect, without external guidance, is also a key strength.

* **Significance:** The problem of entropy collapse in RLVR training is a known limitation, and the proposed SvS strategy offers a practical solution. The empirical results demonstrating substantial improvements in Pass@k performance, particularly on challenging benchmarks like AIME, suggest that the method can effectively push the reasoning boundaries of LLMs. The consistent performance across different model sizes (3B to 32B) strengthens the generalizability of the approach.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-Motivated:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of entropy collapse and its impact on Pass@k.
    * **Novel Approach:**  The SvS strategy is a creative and effective solution to the identified problem.
    * **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental results are compelling, with significant gains in Pass@k performance and consistent improvements across different model sizes and benchmarks.
    * **Thorough Analysis:** The paper provides a good analysis of policy entropy and its relationship to data diversity. The ablations add further support.
    * **Clarity:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and its implementation.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** While the core idea is simple, the implementation details (filtering problems, reward shaping for synthesis) add some complexity to the framework.  A more in-depth analysis into the selection of acc_l and acc_h would be beneficial.
    * **Limited scope of generated variance.** While they focus on preventing a perfect copy of answers within the synthesis, it seems like variance within a single problem would also be ideal to foster diversity among training.
    * **Scalability of Implementation** The paper showcases the generalizability on different scales (from 3B to 32B); it can be said that this is scalable, however the resources spent on performing those operations would also have to be high to have those capabilities in the first place, so the initial bottleneck of generalizability would be its implementation scalability.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to have a significant influence on the field of RLVR training for LLMs. The SvS strategy provides a practical and effective method for mitigating entropy collapse and improving the reasoning capabilities of LLMs. The self-play aspect makes it particularly appealing, as it reduces the reliance on external resources or human annotation. The paper provides a clear direction for future research in this area.

* **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**
    The paper presents a novel and impactful solution to a well-defined problem in the RLVR training of LLMs. The empirical results are convincing and demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed SvS strategy. While the implementation details add some complexity, the core idea is elegant and has the potential to significantly advance the field. Given the significance of addressing entropy collapse and pushing the reasoning boundaries of LLMs, the score reflects a paper of high quality with considerable potential impact.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Unintended Misalignment from Agentic Fine-Tuning: Risks and Mitigation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14031v1)
- **Authors**: Dongyoon Hahm, Taywon Min, Woogyeol Jin, Kimin Lee
- **Abstract**: Beyond simple text generation, Large Language Models (LLMs) have evolved into agentic systems capable of planning and interacting with external tools to solve complex tasks. This evolution involves fine-tuning LLMs on agent-specific tasks to enhance their proficiency. However, safety concerns are frequently overlooked during this fine-tuning process. In this work, we show that aligned LLMs can become unintentionally misaligned, leading to a higher likelihood of executing harmful tasks and a reduced tendency to refuse them when fine-tuned to execute agentic tasks. To address these safety challenges, we propose Prefix INjection Guard (PING), a simple yet effective method that prepends automatically generated natural language prefixes to agent responses, guiding them to refuse harmful requests while preserving performance on benign tasks. Specifically, we introduce an iterative approach that alternates between (1) generating candidate prefixes and (2) selecting those that optimize both task performance and refusal behavior. Experimental results demonstrate that PING significantly enhances the safety of fine-tuned LLM agents without sacrificing their effectiveness. PING consistently outperforms existing prompting approaches across diverse benchmarks in both web navigation and code generation tasks. Our analysis of internal hidden states via linear probes reveals that prefix tokens are crucial for behavior modification, explaining the performance gains. WARNING: This paper contains contents that are unethical or offensive in nature.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Unintended Misalignment from Agentic Fine-Tuning: Risks and Mitigation":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses a critical, often overlooked, safety issue in the development of LLM-based agents. It demonstrates that fine-tuning aligned LLMs on benign, agentic datasets can inadvertently lead to misalignment, increasing the likelihood of the agent executing harmful tasks and reducing its tendency to refuse such requests. To mitigate this, the authors propose Prefix INjection Guard (PING), a method that prepends automatically generated natural language prefixes to agent responses, guiding them to refuse harmful requests while preserving performance on intended tasks. The prefixes are generated and selected through an iterative process that optimizes both task performance and refusal behavior. The experimental results show that PING significantly enhances the safety of fine-tuned LLM agents without sacrificing effectiveness. Analysis of hidden states reveals that the prefix tokens are crucial for behavior modification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in identifying and empirically demonstrating the *unintended* safety risks arising from fine-tuning LLMs for agentic tasks, even on benign datasets. While prior work has explored misalignment and jailbreaking, this paper specifically highlights the subtle dangers introduced by agentic fine-tuning. The PING method is a relatively simple intervention, which, while not fundamentally groundbreaking, is effectively tailored to address the specific problem of misalignment resulting from agentic fine-tuning. The use of activation steering to steer fine-tuned agents is a relatively novel approach in that particular context.

*   **Significance:** The paper is highly significant because LLM agents are rapidly gaining traction and being deployed in real-world applications. The identified safety vulnerabilities present a real and present danger. The PING method offers a practical, lightweight mitigation strategy that can be easily integrated into existing agent development pipelines. The paper correctly identifies how agentic training amplifies potential harm by undermining safety mechanisms.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of unintended misalignment in agentic fine-tuning.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides robust empirical evidence across diverse benchmarks (WebArena-lite, WebDojo, MINT-ALFWorld, RedCode-Exec) and LLMs (Llama-3.1-8B, GLM-4-9B, Qwen2.5-7B, GPT-40-mini, Gemini-2.0-flash).
    *   **Effective Mitigation:** The proposed PING method demonstrates a significant improvement in safety without substantial performance degradation.
    *   **Mechanistic Insights:** The analysis of internal hidden states using linear probes provides valuable insights into how PING works and reinforces the importance of initial tokens in influencing model behavior.
    *   **Layered Safety:** The work clearly demonstrates PING's compatibility with other existing guardrail methods like WildGuard, and further improves safety in conjunction.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Simplicity of PING:** While simplicity is a strength in terms of ease of adoption, it might also limit the potential for PING to address more complex and nuanced safety issues. Other methods might have better generalization performance.
    *   **Prefix Generation Dependency:** The effectiveness of PING relies on the ability of the GENERATOR LLM (GPT-40) to produce effective prefixes. The paper does not thoroughly explore the limitations and biases of the generator.
    *   **Limited scope of harmful tasks:** The paper uses a specific set of harmful tasks. While this is necessary for evaluation, there's no definitive proof or statement on the generalization performance of this method.
    *   **Dependency on linear probes:** The paper relies heavily on linear probes as a mechanistic explaination. There is still some discussion within the interpretability community about the validity of linear probes as a mechanistic explanation.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper is likely to influence the development and deployment of LLM agents by raising awareness of the potential safety risks and providing a practical mitigation strategy. It may also inspire further research into more sophisticated methods for ensuring the safety and alignment of agentic systems.

*   **Rigorous Justification for assigned score:**
    The assignment of the score is not arbitrary, but based on:
    1. Clear identification of the problem and demonstration with strong experimental results.
    2. Proposition of a concrete, working method.
    3. Explaination and intuition based on linear probes.
    4. Shortcomings in terms of novelty and explainability of the approach.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:** The paper makes a valuable contribution by highlighting an important, subtle safety risk in LLM agent development. The PING method is practical and effective, and the analysis of internal representations provides some mechanistic insights. However, the simplicity of PING and its reliance on another LLM for prefix generation, limit the paper's overall novelty and potential long-term impact. While the activation steering techniques provide some indication of mechanistic influence, the approach isn't entirely satisfying.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### The Promise of Large Language Models in Digital Health: Evidence from Sentiment Analysis in Online Health Communities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.14032v1)
- **Authors**: Xiancheng Li, Georgios D. Karampatakis, Helen E. Wood, Chris J. Griffiths, Borislava Mihaylova, Neil S. Coulson, Alessio Pasinato, Pietro Panzarasa, Marco Viviani, Anna De Simoni
- **Abstract**: Digital health analytics face critical challenges nowadays. The sophisticated analysis of patient-generated health content, which contains complex emotional and medical contexts, requires scarce domain expertise, while traditional ML approaches are constrained by data shortage and privacy limitations in healthcare settings. Online Health Communities (OHCs) exemplify these challenges with mixed-sentiment posts, clinical terminology, and implicit emotional expressions that demand specialised knowledge for accurate Sentiment Analysis (SA). To address these challenges, this study explores how Large Language Models (LLMs) can integrate expert knowledge through in-context learning for SA, providing a scalable solution for sophisticated health data analysis. Specifically, we develop a structured codebook that systematically encodes expert interpretation guidelines, enabling LLMs to apply domain-specific knowledge through targeted prompting rather than extensive training. Six GPT models validated alongside DeepSeek and LLaMA 3.1 are compared with pre-trained language models (BioBERT variants) and lexicon-based methods, using 400 expert-annotated posts from two OHCs. LLMs achieve superior performance while demonstrating expert-level agreement. This high agreement, with no statistically significant difference from inter-expert agreement levels, suggests knowledge integration beyond surface-level pattern recognition. The consistent performance across diverse LLM models, supported by in-context learning, offers a promising solution for digital health analytics. This approach addresses the critical challenge of expert knowledge shortage in digital health research, enabling real-time, expert-quality analysis for patient monitoring, intervention assessment, and evidence-based health strategies.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of Large Language Models (LLMs) for sentiment analysis (SA) in online health communities (OHCs), addressing challenges like data scarcity and the need for domain expertise. The authors introduce a structured codebook approach to systematically encode expert interpretation guidelines, enabling LLMs to apply domain-specific knowledge through targeted prompting rather than extensive training. They compare the performance of GPT models, along with DeepSeek and LLaMA, against pre-trained language models (BioBERT variants) and lexicon-based methods using expert-annotated data from two health communities. The results demonstrate that LLMs achieve superior performance and expert-level agreement. Confidence calibration analysis further reveals that certain LLMs provide reliable uncertainty estimates, supporting quality-controlled deployment.  The authors provide an open-source implementation to encourage wider adoption.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a compelling combination of several elements, contributing to its overall novelty.

    *   The **structured codebook approach** is a novel and systematic way to encode and transfer expert knowledge into LLM prompts, moving beyond simple task instructions.
    *   The **focus on OHCs** as a complex and challenging test bed for SA, given the mixed sentiment, clinical terminology, and nuanced expressions, is a significant differentiator from studies focusing on general social media data or simplified health contexts like tweets.
    *   The **emphasis on confidence calibration** in the context of healthcare is a crucial and often overlooked aspect. The demonstration that certain LLMs can provide reliable uncertainty estimates is particularly valuable for practical deployment.
    *   The **comparative analysis of multiple LLM architectures**, including open-source models like DeepSeek and LLaMA, adds to the generalizability and applicability of the findings.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in digital health analytics: the scarcity of domain expertise and annotated data. The structured prompting methodology provides a scalable solution for sophisticated analysis, enabling real-time, expert-quality insights for patient monitoring, intervention assessment, and evidence-based health strategies. The open-source implementation greatly enhances the potential for practical adoption.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous methodology:**  The study employs a comprehensive comparative evaluation using multiple models, datasets, and performance metrics. The inclusion of inter-annotator agreement and confidence calibration adds depth to the analysis.
    *   **Practical focus:** The paper emphasizes the practical implications of the research, addressing deployment concerns like data security and privacy. The open-source implementation and detailed codebook provide valuable resources for researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Clear and well-articulated:** The paper presents the problem, methodology, results, and conclusions in a clear and concise manner.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope limitations:** The evaluation is primarily focused on SA in OHCs. While this is a valuable starting point, the generalizability of the structured prompting approach to other healthcare text analysis tasks requires further investigation.
    *   **Codebook complexity:** The creation of a comprehensive codebook requires significant expertise and effort.  The study acknowledges this limitation and encourages the development of standardized approaches for codebook development.
    *   **Fine-tuning exploration:** The study primarily focuses on zero-shot and few-shot learning. Future research could explore the potential benefits of fine-tuning LLMs for specific healthcare applications while considering privacy-preserving techniques.
    *   **Ethics considerations:** The paper acknowledges the ethical implications of using LLMs in healthcare, but it could benefit from a more in-depth discussion of potential biases, fairness concerns, and the need for human oversight, particularly in high-stakes decision-making contexts.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of digital health analytics by providing a practical and scalable approach for integrating expert knowledge into LLMs. The structured prompting methodology and open-source implementation can accelerate the adoption of advanced text analysis capabilities in resource-constrained healthcare settings. The emphasis on confidence calibration is also likely to encourage further research on uncertainty quantification and quality control in LLM-based healthcare applications.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to digital health analytics. The structured codebook approach, the focus on OHCs, and the emphasis on confidence calibration are valuable contributions. While the paper has some limitations regarding scope and the complexity of codebook creation, its strengths in terms of rigorous methodology, practical focus, and potential influence outweigh these weaknesses. This warrants an 8, reflecting a significant advancement in the field with demonstrable practical value and scope for further research and development.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10
